ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION FOR
FINITE-GROUP EXTENSIONS OF SHIFTS OF FINITE
TYPE
HAOBO MA
Abstract. For a mixing subshift of finite type carrying a finite-group
cocycle, we study how twisted finite-dimensional determinants determine primitive orbit data by conjugacy class. Periodic labels record powers of primitive labels, so passing from periodic determinant logarithms
to primitive label series requires Adams operations g 7→ g m together
with Möbius inversion. This gives an explicit Peter–Weyl determinant
formula for the primitive channels. Under the strict twisted spectral gap
η < λ, these channels have Perron-point values and hence class-indexed
constant terms. We use the classical Frobenius-class Euler-product exponent |C|/|G| as input and recover the Adams-corrected product constants from the fixed-label Euler transform, not from the periodic Artin
logarithm. We prove the corresponding determinant rigidity and a finite
S3 certificate in which the explicit values Fε (1/2) and Fρ (1/2) obstruct
naive Artin substitution at the product-constant level. The scalar finitepart and canonical cyclic-lift calculations are used only as finite-matrix
normalisation and comparison tools; they are separate from the finitegroup determinant and product theorems.

1. Introduction
The problem addressed in this paper is primitive orbit reconstruction for
+
, σ) be a
finite-group extensions of mixing subshifts of finite type. Let (XA
mixing subshift with primitive adjacency matrix A, let λ = ρ(A), and let τ :
EA → G be a cocycle into a finite group. For every irreducible representation
ϱ of G, the twisted transition matrix Aϱ records periodic labels through its
traces. These traces are periodic data. The primitive labels are subtler:
when a primitive orbit with label g is repeated m times, its label becomes
g m . Thus primitive extraction is not ordinary Möbius inversion in the nonabelian setting; it must be coupled to the Adams operations ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m )
on class functions.
The centre of the paper is the Adams–Möbius passage from twisted dynamical determinants to primitive Peter–Weyl channels and then to the
constants in Frobenius-class Euler products. The classical Frobenius-class
Mertens asymptotic supplies the exponent |C|/|G|:
PC (N ) = KC N −|C|/|G| (1 + o(1)).

The finite-state problem addressed here is the constant KC . Under the strict
twisted gap it is governed by the fixed-label Euler transform

not by the periodic Artin logarithm Lϱ . Replacing Fϱ by Lϱ is already false
in the finite S3 model of Appendix C. The contribution is therefore the
Adams-corrected fixed-label determinant formula for KC , its finite recovery,
and the obstruction to Artin substitution; it is not a new claim about the
classical class-Mertens exponent.
The finite-group argument has three steps: Main Theorem I linearises
twisted determinants and performs primitive Adams–Möbius inversion;
Main Theorem II evaluates the resulting non-trivial primitive series at
z = λ−1 , after imposing the twisted spectral gap η < λ, and obtains
conjugacy-class Mertens constants; Main Theorem III identifies the
Euler-product constants, while the Fourier recovery of the corresponding
Perron-point primitive channels is a structural corollary. The recovery
of these constants is the Hermitian character-orthogonality formula. Its
forward class coefficient is χ∗ϱ (C) := χϱ (C); for unitary characters this is
equivalently χϱ (C −1 ).
The scalar finite part of ζA and the cyclic root-of-unity layer have narrower
roles. The finite part supplies only the trivial-channel normalisation M(A),
while root-of-unity sampling belongs only to the canonical cyclic lift, where
every edge is labelled by the generator of Z/qZ. It is not the same as taking
arbitrary cyclic quotients of the fixed cocycle. The logical direction of the
proofs is

been constructed. The formal input–output statements and the scalar normalisation are collected in the next subsection.
The formal theorem statements below summarize this argument before
the technical development begins. Main Theorem I is the determinant-toprimitive Adams–Möbius inversion. Main Theorem II adds the strict twisted
gap and evaluates the non-trivial primitive channels at the Perron point.
Main Theorem III inserts those values into the Frobenius-class Euler product and recovers the corrected class constants. The later rigidity, quotient,
cyclic-detection, and certificate statements serve this chain by testing hypotheses or computing its ingredients in finite models; in particular, the
eta-gap obstruction is used only to certify the strict Perron-point input of
Main Theorems II–III, not to classify all finite-group cocycles with a strict

Proof. The product asymptotic and the displayed formula for KC are precisely the conclusion of Main Theorem III, proved in the body as Theorem 3.38. Taking logarithms of its constant formula gives the displayed class
transform. The determinant recovery of each Fϱ (λ−1 ) is Proposition 3.56,
equivalently the finite coordinate form in Theorem 3.41. Finally, the irreducible characters form an orthonormal basis of class functions; applying the
inverse formula of Corollary 3.46 to the difference between the fixed-label
and periodic-Artin vectors proves that equality of all resulting class constants is equivalent to vanishing of every non-trivial coordinate difference.
This is exactly the comparison isolated later in Theorems 3.49 and 3.63. □
The scalar finite part of ζA , the cyclic-lift comparison calculations, and
the finite certificates in the appendices are supporting material rather than
a second route to the product constants. The scalar calculation supplies the
trivial-channel normalisation M(A). The cyclic lift is a fixed-matrix comparison model and records a no-descent boundary; it is not an invariant of arbitrary cyclic quotients of the cocycle. The optional secondary spectral-edge
appendix is downstream from the main finite-group spine; its only role in the
body is the non-interference gate Proposition 3.61, which says that auxiliary
compensated tails satisfying the stated vanishing condition cannot change
Πϱ (λ−1 ), MC (A, τ ; G), Fϱ (λ−1 ), or KC . Thus the proofs of Main Theorems I–III use only finite-matrix determinant identities, finite-group character orthogonality, Adams–Möbius inversion, and Perron–Frobenius theory
for finite non-negative matrices; no Hölder Banach-space transfer theorem,
Ruelle resonance theorem, pressure-window theorem, completed-zeta or xi
normalisation, universality hierarchy, or external determinant-normalisation
theorem is a premise of the paper.

For reference, the proof dependencies of the three main statements are as
follows. Main Theorem I is the finite determinant linearisation and Adams–
Möbius primitive inversion of Lemma 3.6, theorems 3.7, 3.20 and 3.23,
and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18. Main Theorem II adds the strict Perron-point
gap, whose finite certificate, Perron-disc analytic separation, and explicitly
conditional scope are Propositions 4.6, 4.7 and 4.10, and is proved by Theorems 3.32 and 3.68 and proposition 3.66. Main Theorem III is the productlevel assembly from Theorems 3.38, 3.49 and 3.63 and propositions 3.56
and 3.58; the concrete S3 obstruction is certified by Proposition C.4 and lemmas C.5 and D.2 through the same finite determinant certificate and does
not introduce an additional hypothesis.

extension of the primitive edge shift fixed in this paper, Main Theorems I–

contains no hidden premise consisting of a Hölder/Ruelle transfer operator

For |z| < λ−1 the identity log det(I − zAϱ )−1 = n≥1 Tr(Anϱ )z n /n is therefore the ordinary finite-matrix determinant expansion. The coefficient identity relating Tr(Anϱ ) to periodic skew-product weights is exactly Lemma 3.6.
Applying the finite character orthogonality of G gives the class-function linearisation of Theorem 3.7; applying the Adams operations ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m )
and scalar Möbius inversion gives Corollary 3.10. Thus the primitive determinant and trace recoveries in Main Theorem I are precisely Corollaries 3.18

The Perron-boundary constants in Main Theorems II and III are
evaluated only after imposing the explicit finite-dimensional hypothesis
η = maxϱ̸=1 ρ(Aϱ ) < λ. Under this hypothesis, Theorems 3.32 and 3.68
and proposition 3.66 give the additive class constants from the same determinant logarithms, while Theorems 3.38, 3.49 and 3.63 and proposition 3.56
give the product constants and the Adams-corrected obstruction. The
equivalence between the displayed gap and the peripheral statement for the
finite skew-product adjacency matrix is Lemma 3.4. These cited results
exhaust the dependencies listed immediately before the proposition. None
invokes a Banach-space transfer operator, a Ruelle resonance theorem, a
completed zeta function, or a functional equation, so no such theorem is a
hidden input to Main Theorems I–III.
□
The paper’s main contribution is recorded in the following three numbered
headline theorems. Main Theorem I is the open-disc determinant inversion
theorem: it converts periodic twisted determinants into primitive Peter–
Weyl channels before any Perron-boundary evaluation. Main Theorem II is
the Perron-point class-constant theorem: after the strict twisted gap, it evaluates those primitive channels at z = λ−1 and gives the additive Frobeniusclass Mertens constants. Main Theorem III is the Adams-corrected symbolicdynamics novelty of the paper: it identifies the Adams-corrected fixed-label
Euler-product constants and proves that the ordinary periodic Artin logarithm is not the right primitive class-product substitute. Thus the paper is positioned in the symbolic dynamics and dynamical-zeta literature
by source role rather than by a blanket claim of new prime-orbit asymptotics. Parry–Pollicott’s monograph and Chebotarev work give the transferoperator, Artin–Ruelle determinant, and Galois-cover orbit-counting framework [32, 31]. Noorani–Parry give the finite homogeneous-extension Chebotarev theorem for shifts [27]. Sharp supplies the closed-orbit Mertens
model and homology-class orbit technology [41, 42], while Mesliza–Noorani
give the Frobenius-class Mertens comparison theorem [24]. Those papers
account for the leading density, the exponent |C|/|G|, and the existence
layer of Frobenius-class products. They do not contain the finite-label
Adams–Möbius Euler transform used here to pass from periodic determinant logarithms to primitive fixed-label product constants. Recent published ETDS papers by Coles, Humbert, O’Hare, and Crismale–Del Vecchio–
Griseta–Rossi are used only to calibrate the present symbolic-dynamics setting against current work on periodic-orbit invariants, Ruelle resonances,
finite-data rigidity, and non-commutative skew products [8, 17, 28, 9]; they
are not proof inputs. The new material here is the fixed-label Adams–Möbius
Euler transform, its finite determinant recovery, the Hermitian class transform, and the S3 obstruction showing that the periodic Artin logarithm gives
the wrong product constant.
This positioning leaves a narrow new contribution. Parry–Pollicott’s
Artin–Ruelle and Chebotarev framework, Noorani–Parry’s finite-extension
Chebotarev theorem, Sharp’s closed-orbit Mertens theorem, and Mesliza–
Noorani’s Frobenius-class Mertens product theorem supply the classical

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

7

orbit-counting background: the leading density, the exponent |C|/|G|, and
the existence layer of class products. The additional finite-state result proved
here is the Adams–Möbius passage from periodic determinant logarithms to
primitive fixed-label Peter–Weyl channels, followed by determinant recovery
of the additive and product constants. In particular, Main Theorem III
keeps the classical exponent but identifies the non-scalar coordinate of the
product constant as the fixed-label Euler value Fϱ (λ−1 ), proves its determinant formula in Proposition 3.56 and theorem 3.41, and separates it from the
periodic Artin logarithm through Proposition 3.37 and theorem 3.63. Recent published work of Coles, Humbert, O’Hare, and Crismale–Del Vecchio–
Griseta–Rossi marks neighbouring activity on periodic-orbit observables, Ruelle resonances, finite-data rigidity, and non-commutative skew products;
those papers are comparison points rather than inputs to the proof chain.
Secondary-edge asymptotics are likewise downstream from the three main
theorems and are not used to identify any constant; the body records only
the non-interference gate Proposition 3.61, while any sharper tail expansion
is confined to the optional appendix.
Theorem 1.3 (Main Theorem I: finite determinant recovery of primitive
channels). Let A be primitive and let τ : EA → G be a finite-group edge
cocycle. For every class function ϕ : G → C, the primitive label series
Πϕ (z) on |z| < λ−1 is determined by the finite family of twisted determinant
polynomials

Proof. This is precisely Proposition 3.1. That proposition first invokes the
twisted trace identity and the determinant logarithm expansion to obtain
the periodic series Lϕ , then applies Adams–Möbius inversion to pass from
periodic labels to primitive labels, and finally uses Peter–Weyl inversion to
recover the class counts. These are exactly the operations displayed in the
statement, so no additional finite-group hypothesis or limiting orbit datum
is being imported here.

The factor |C|/|G| multiplies the entire Hermitian class-indicator bracket,
including the non-trivial fixed-label sum. Each Fϱ (λ−1 ) is computable from
the same finite twisted determinant data as Main Theorem I by substituting
the Adams–Möbius determinant formula into the displayed series. Thus the
leading exponent and the existence of such Frobenius-class products are the
classical part of the statement; the new assertion is the primitive-label fixedEuler determinant recovery of KC and the resulting obstruction to replacing
Fϱ by the periodic Artin logarithm. As a downstream structural refinement,
the non-trivial Peter–Weyl boundary is a rank-sharp coordinate record only
for the scalar-normalised product constants in fixed branch-compatible determinant coordinates: Corollary 3.45 proves that every real linear record,
after the scalar baseline is fixed, determining all branch-compatible product constants has rank at least the number of non-trivial conjugacy-class
degrees of freedom, and that the Adams-corrected determinant coordinates
attain this bound. It is not asserted as a universal minimality theorem for
arbitrary observations of the extension, nor as a branch-free determinant
statement. This distinction is part of the statement: the exponent |C|/|G|
and the existence of Frobenius-class Mertens products belong to the Parry–
Pollicott, Noorani–Parry, Sharp, and Mesliza–Noorani orbit-counting lineage cited above; the novelty asserted here begins only after that classical
leading term has been separated, namely at the Adams–Möbius fixed-label
determinant formula for the product constant.
Proof. The fixed-label Euler transform is established in Theorem 3.11; applying it class by class gives the product asymptotic and the displayed expression for KC in Theorem 3.38. The claim that every value Fϱ (λ−1 ), and hence
every KC , is recovered from the finite twisted determinant data is proved
in the finite-determinant recovery package Propositions 3.56 and 3.58, theorem 3.41, and corollary 3.43. The scalar-normalised coordinate and rank
statements are precisely Corollary 3.45, while the comparison with the periodic Artin logarithm is Theorems 3.49 and 3.63. Thus the proof uses only
the chain cited in the statement; the finite S3 certificates enter later only as
explicit witnesses to the obstruction and not as hypotheses for the product
theorem.

HAOBO MA

The product constants are unaffected by the retained conditional
secondary-edge refinement: Propositions 3.61 and F.4 record that any finite
secondary refinement whose compensated tails vanish leaves MC (A, τ ; G),
KC , and the recovered values Fϱ (λ−1 ) unchanged. The appendix therefore
supplies a conditional refinement of the error term and a detection criterion,
rather than a fourth main theorem or an alternative proof of the product
constants.
The corollary-level refinements Corollaries 3.45 and 3.64 then assemble
the already proved constants into a fixed-coordinate Peter–Weyl coordinate
vector and a Parseval obstruction bound. They are used below only as
structural consequences and finite witnesses for the scalar-normalised product constants, not as observation-system minimality claims or as additional
parts of Main Theorem III.
Proof guide. The trace identity underlying Main Theorem I is Lemma 3.6;
the determinant linearisation and Adams–Möbius primitive inversion are
Theorem 3.7 and corollary 3.10. The determinant-germ formula and coefficient forms are Corollary 3.18 and theorems 3.20 and 3.23. Main Theorem II
is proved by Theorems 3.32 and 3.68 and proposition 3.66. Main Theorem III
is Theorem 3.38. The determinant evaluation in Proposition 3.56 and corollary 3.40 identifies the constant without adding hypotheses to the main
theorems. The eta-gap diagnostic Theorem 4.1 explains when the strict-gap
hypothesis fails for the fixed finite-state cocycle; its one-dimensional loopcharacter and perfect-loop-group consequences are Corollaries 4.3 and 4.5.
These results certify the Perron-point hypothesis but are not separate mechanisms for product constants or a classification of all strict-gap cocycles.
Secondary spectral-edge refinements are downstream from this proof
guide and are retained in the appendix only under the additional separation
hypotheses stated there. The core protection against leakage is the finitetail non-interference proposition Proposition 3.61: once the determinant
coordinates and product constants have been fixed by the main chain,
any auxiliary finite spectral edge whose compensated tail tends to zero
contributes no new Perron-boundary coordinate, no new Mertens constant,
and no new Euler-product constant. Any sharper secondary-tail expansion,
isolation statement, or dominant-modulus detection result remains confined
to the optional appendix and supplies no hypothesis or constant used by
Main Theorems I–III.

the Peter–Weyl decomposition splits the Perron-point class profile into its
one-dimensional sector and its higher-dimensional sector. The span of all
cyclic quotient observables is exactly the one-dimensional sector; therefore
its projection is the abelian shadow, orthogonal to the genuine non-abelian
defect. Under η < λ, the full primitive class profile is generated by cyclic
quotient shadows exactly when every higher-dimensional primitive channel

Similarly, a scalar record formed only from the fixed matrix A, such
as FP(A), M(A), the reduced polynomial FA , the branch-lifted cyclic-lift
record {Ψq (A)}q≥1 , or a torsion layer {FA (ωqj )}0≤j<q , cannot by itself recover the non-trivial finite-group profile for a family of cocycles over the
same base matrix. The precise projection boundary is Theorem 5.2: scalar
data determine a single normalised class profile across such a family exactly
when all non-trivial Perron-point channels Πτϱ (λ−1 ), ϱ ̸= 1, are already independent of τ . Thus the scalar finite-part and cyclic-lift statements below
are fixed-matrix inputs and do not enlarge the finite-group determinant and
product results; the two-cocycle separation is Corollary 5.3.
An explicit S3 -extension separates the two obstruction types at the level
relevant to Main Theorem III. Its fixed-label Euler-transform certificates
Fε (1/2) ∈ (−381/1000, −380/1000) and Fρ (1/2) ∈ (−923/1000, −922/1000)
force the product-constant obstruction to naive Artin substitution. The accompanying primitive certificates show that the sign-character trace vanishes
but its primitive value is non-zero by the quadratic Adams mechanism, while
the standard two-dimensional channel gives the certified higher-dimensional
cyclic detection defect. This is a finite-state obstruction model, not a new
periodic-orbit distribution theorem.
The quotient/shadow consequence is proved in Theorems 5.2, 5.4, 5.8,
5.10 and 5.14, corollaries 5.3 and 5.5, and proposition 5.6.

Corollary 1.7 (Introductory quotient-shadow consequences). Under the
twisted spectral gap, the body proves a quotient-shadow theorem description
of the normalised primitive class profile. It includes scalar projection onto
the one-dimensional Peter–Weyl sector, functoriality for finite quotients, the
orthogonal decomposition into abelian shadow and genuine non-abelian defect, and exact detection of the one-dimensional sector by cyclic quotients
of the fixed cocycle. In particular, the one-dimensional shadow is obtained
by pulling back the primitive profile of the abelianised cocycle. If a further
cyclic record is desired, it is the separate canonical cyclic-lift model, whose
channels are the root-of-unity determinant layers log det(I − ωqj zA)−1 , not
an invariant of an arbitrary cyclic quotient of the fixed cocycle.
Proof. This is the front-matter summary of the body results in Theorems 5.4
and 5.8, corollary 5.5, and lemmas 5.7 and 5.9. The quotient theorem gives
the functorial finite-quotient formula for primitive profiles. The abelianshadow corollary proves that the one-dimensional characters of G are precisely the pullbacks of the characters of Gab , that the twisted gap descends
to the abelianised cocycle, and that substituting the resulting Perron-point
primitive channels into the normalised class-profile formula gives the pullback identity (128). The defect theorem records the orthogonal split between the abelian and higher-dimensional sectors, while the cyclic-quotient
lemma identifies the span of all cyclic quotient coordinates with the onedimensional character span. Finally, Lemma 5.7 separates inherited cyclic
quotient data from the canonical cyclic lift τq (e) = 1, for which the twisted
matrices are ωqj A and the determinant channels are the displayed root-ofunity layers. Thus the quotient/shadow statement is a proved structural
consequence supporting Main Theorems II–III, not an informal corollary or
an additional descent principle.
□
This is the trivial-channel scalar input singled out in Section 1.2; the scalar
statement above is proved in Proposition 1.6, lemma 6.1, and corollary 6.3.
The second scalar component is the separate fixed-matrix lift model, related to inverse spectral questions for SFTs [21]. Appendix A records the
branch-lifted reconstruction and one-layer Fourier recovery for this fixed
matrix comparison problem; the stable synopsis labels are Theorems A.7
and A.8; these are appendix-support labels, not additional main theorem
labels. As already specified in Section 1.2, those results are not used in the

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

13

finite-group determinant recovery or product-constant proofs; they serve
only to prevent the scalar cyclic-lift record from being mistaken for a nonabelian class-profile invariant. A broader cyclic-lift inverse-spectral or identifiability theory would require separate hypotheses and examples; it is not
developed here. The terminology of cyclic, torsion, and exponential spectral
records is fixed in Definition 2.8.
1.4. Relation to existing work. The natural comparison class is the
symbolic-dynamics and dynamical ζ-function literature on prime cycles, finite extensions, and twisted determinant factorizations. In that literature
the leading Chebotarev distribution and Frobenius-class product asymptotics are classical inputs. Parry–Pollicott’s Chebotarev theorem for Axiom A Galois coverings [31], Noorani–Parry’s finite homogeneous-extension
theorem for shifts [27], Sharp’s Mertens theorem for Axiom A flows [41], and
Mesliza–Noorani’s Frobenius-class Mertens product theorem [24] supply the
background against which Main Theorems II–III should be read. The exponent |C|/|G| and the existence of Frobenius-class products are therefore not
claimed as new here. The theorem-local contribution is the finite Adams–
Möbius determinant recovery of the class constants after that classical leading term has been separated.
At the determinant level, the paper is closer to Fried’s twisted periodic
point formulae [13, 12], the twisted Perron–Frobenius framework of Adachi–
Sunada [1], and graph-Artin determinant theories of Hashimoto, Sunada,
Stark–Terras, and related finite-graph covering work [14, 15, 16, 47, 44,
45, 46]. Those works encode periodic or prime-cycle data by determinants
or representation-theoretic L-functions. The present finite-state calculation
fixes one finite-group SFT cocycle and asks the inverse question needed for
the constants: from the full family of twisted determinants, recover the primitive Peter–Weyl channels. The extra operation is the finite-group Adams
correction ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m ), forced by repetitions of a primitive orbit.
Finite group actions and extensions of SFTs form the adjacent symbolic
setting. Adler–Kitchens–Marcus [2], Fiebig [11], Silver–Williams [43], Boyle–
Schmieding [7], Boyle–Carlsen–Eilers [6], and Matsumoto [23] treat finite
actions, quotients, periodic data, and classification or obstruction questions.
Here the extension is fixed; the issue is not existence or flow-equivalence
classification but determinant-level recovery of Πϱ (λ−1 ), MC (A, τ ; G), and
the Adams-corrected Euler-product constants.
Recent published work gives contextual comparison points in nearby dynamical systems settings. Coles [8] studies geometric periodic-orbit observables for Axiom A flows; Humbert [17] studies Ruelle-resonance phenomena; O’Hare [28] treats finite-data rigidity in a different dynamical setting; and Crismale–Del Vecchio–Griseta–Rossi [9] address operator-algebraic
skew-product dynamics. These are comparison points, not proof inputs. The
result isolated below is the fixed-label Euler transform
X Πϱ (z r )
,
Fϱ (z) =
r
r≥1

14

HAOBO MA

its Adams-corrected determinant expression, and the obstruction to replacing it by the periodic Artin logarithm Lϱ in Frobenius-class product constants.
The recent comparison works cited above locate the present contribution
but are not inputs to the proofs. The geometric periodic-orbit observables
in [8], the Ruelle-resonance analysis in [17], the finite-data rigidity theorem
of [28], and the operator-algebraic skew-product framework of [9] address
neighbouring dynamical questions with different outputs. In contrast, this
paper fixes one finite SFT cocycle and proves the determinant recovery of
primitive Peter–Weyl channels, the Perron-point class Mertens constants,
and the Adams-corrected fixed-label Euler-product constants. The quotient,
shadow, eta-gap, and S3 statements later in the paper are supporting corollaries and finite certificates: they explain functoriality, scalar no-descent, the
boundary of the strict twisted gap, and a concrete obstruction to replacing
Fϱ by Lϱ . They are not separate classification theorems competing with
Main Theorems I–III.
1.5. Plan of the paper. Section 2 fixes notation for primitive orbit counts,
reduced determinants, cyclic lifts, and finite-group extensions. The core
argument is in Section 3: the Adams–Möbius determinant recovery gives the
primitive Peter–Weyl channels, the Perron evaluation gives the Frobeniusclass Mertens constants, and the same finite data give the Adams-corrected
product constants. Section 4 isolates the strict eta-gap certificate required
for those constants, and Section 5 records the quotient and shadow structure
that explains which scalar or cyclic data are insufficient. Section 6 then
places the scalar finite part and fixed-matrix cyclic-lift comparison inside
that scope.
The appendices collect supporting finite-state computations and their role
is described once in the dependency table. The final appendix is optional conditional secondary-edge material after the main constants have been proved;
its only connection with the theorem spine is the non-interference statement
Proposition 3.61, so it can be omitted without changing Main Theorems I–
III. Appendices A and B support the scalar finite-part and cyclic-lift comparisons used around the finite-part normalisation. Appendices C and D
supply the finite S3 matrices and printed rational inequalities used only as
the concrete witness for the Artin-substitution obstruction in Main Theorem III.
2. Preliminaries
The notation in this section follows the hierarchy of the paper. We first
fix primitive orbit counts for the base shift, then the finite-group extension
and Adams operations used in the main theorem chain. The reduced determinant, finite part, and cyclic-lift notation come afterwards because they
serve only as scalar normalisation and canonical cyclic-lift comparison data.
Finite-data guide. All main objects are finite. The base datum is a
primitive adjacency matrix A of a finite edge shift, with Perron root λ =
ρ(A) > 0. A cocycle τ : EA → G assigns labels in a finite group. For
ϱ ∈ Irr(G), the twisted matrix Aϱ is the finite block transition matrix whose

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

15

traces record periodic labels in the ϱ-channel, and
η := max ρ(Aϱ )
ϱ̸=1

is the non-trivial twisted spectral radius, with the empty maximum read as
0. The strict inequality η < λ is an additional finite-matrix hypothesis in
Main Theorems II and III; it is not inferred from Main Theorem I. For a
class function ϕ, Πϕ (z) denotes the primitive fixed-label series, Lϕ (z) the
periodic Artin logarithm, and
X Πϕ (z r )
Fϕ (z) =
r
r≥1
the fixed-label Euler transform. The distinction between Fϕ and Lϕ is the
Adams distinction: repetitions change labels by g 7→ g r . The Adams operation is ψ r ϕ(g) = ϕ(g r ), and the Adams–Möbius coefficients are the finite
character-table coefficients obtained when each ψ r χπ is expanded in the
irreducible characters.
The Fourier convention is Hermitian. The forward class coefficient is
χ∗ϱ (C) := χϱ (C) = χϱ (C −1 ), while inverse recovery uses χσ (C) without
conjugation. The scalar normalisations are C(A) = det′ (I − A/λ)−1 , FP(A),
and M(A) = γ + FP(A). The trivial determinant det(I − zA) is singular
at z = λ−1 , so the trivial channel is never evaluated there as an ordinary
determinant branch; it enters only through the finite part M(A) and the
reduced constant C(A). Non-trivial channels may be evaluated at z = λ−1
only after the strict gap η < λ has made their determinant branches regular.

These are auxiliary scalar records of this one matrix A. They are not invariants of a varying family and not class-profile data for a finite-group cocycle

profile, a cyclic-lift invariant, or a stable invariant under changes of matrix
presentation.
2.5. Standing notation. Throughout the paper ωq = e2πi/q denotes a
primitive qth root of unity, and Φq the qth cyclotomic polynomial. We
reserve the notation ϱ for irreducible representations of finite groups and
keep ρ(·) for spectral radii. Sections 3 and 5 use the graph and cocycle
picture for finite-group extensions. The later scalar section, Section 6, uses
only the primitive matrix formalism and cyclic lifts.
3. Adams–Möbius primitive inversion for finite-group
extensions
This section proves the finite-group primitive inversion results. Twisted
matrices Aϱ first linearise periodic data in Peter–Weyl coordinates; the
Adams–Möbius identity then removes repetitions of primitive orbits, where
labels change from g to g m . We use standard character-ring and Adamsoperation formalism as in [4, 40, 19], together with the classical Witt-vector
and necklace formalism for passing from repeated to primitive data [10, 25,
48].
The proof is organised in two labelled blocks and several bridge statements. Only the first block is part of Main Theorem I. The block Lemma 3.6,
theorem 3.7, and corollaries 3.10, 3.18 and 3.21, is the Adams–Möbius determinant and coefficient mechanism and does not use a twisted spectral
gap. The second block, Theorems 3.32 and 3.68 and proposition 3.66, belongs to Main Theorem II and starts only after the twisted spectral gap
η = maxϱ̸=1 ρ(Aϱ ) < λ is imposed and converts the primitive channels
into the class-indexed constants MC (A, τ ; G), from which the channels are
read back by non-abelian character orthogonality. The intervening classdecomposition formulas are bridge statements between these two blocks,
not extra headline theorems.
Throughout the section, the underlying finite-state data are the data fixed
in Proposition 2.5; the additional boundary assumptions λ > 1 and η < λ
are invoked only in the statements where Perron-point values or Mertens
constants are evaluated.
3.1. Main Theorem I: Adams–Möbius primitive determinant
linearisation. The first main block has five steps. The twisted trace
identity converts matrices into periodic Peter–Weyl sums. The determinant
linearisation rewrites those sums as logarithms of twisted determinants.
The Adams–Möbius inversion then removes repetitions of primitive orbits, and the irreducible-channel formula rewrites the result entirely in
terms of the determinant data. Finally, coefficient extraction gives the

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

23

primitive Peter–Weyl trace formula. The preliminary class-indicator and
block-diagonalisation propositions below set up this mechanism; they are
not separate main results.
For reference, the logical dependency map for this block is
twisted traces → determinant linearisation
→ Adams–Möbius inversion → irreducible channels
→ class-count recovery.
The next proposition records the assembled finite construction with its hypotheses and outputs, so that the introduction, the Peter–Weyl bridge, and
the later Mertens section all refer to a single proved interface rather than to
an informal chain of aliases.
Proposition 3.1 (Main Theorem I finite determinant interface). The following implications assemble Main Theorem I without any use of the Perronboundary hypothesis η < λ.
(a) Lemma 3.6 and theorem 3.7 identify, for every class function ϕ, the periodic logarithm Lϕ (z) on |z| < λ−1 as a finite character-table linear combination of the determinant logarithms log det(I − zAπ )−1 , π ∈ Irr(G).
(b) Corollary 3.10 applies the Adams power maps in the character ring to
recover the primitive class series Πϕ (z) from those periodic determinant
logarithms.
(c) Corollary 3.18 and theorem 3.20 specialize the preceding identity to irreducible characters and give, respectively, the determinant-germ formula
and the coefficient formula for every primitive Peter–Weyl channel.
(d) Theorem 3.23 applies finite character orthogonality to those channels
and gives the primitive Frobenius-class counts from the same finite determinant and Adams data.
Consequently Main Theorem I is exactly the finite-state determinant construction consisting of the twisted matrices Aπ , the finite character table of
G, and the power maps on conjugacy classes.
Proof. Part (a) is the content of Lemma 3.6 and theorem 3.7: the trace identity expresses periodic labelled traces
in Peter–Weyl coordinates, and the
P
usual identity log det(I − zAπ )−1 = n≥1 Tr(Anπ )z n /n converts those traces
into determinant logarithms in the open Perron disc. Part (b) is exactly
Corollary 3.10; its proof is Möbius inversion of the primitive expansion with
the label changed by g 7→ g m , hence by the Adams operation ψ m . Part (c)
follows by substituting ϕ = χϱ and then either leaving the identity as a
determinant germ or extracting coefficients. The Adams coefficients are the
(m)
finite inner products aϱ,π of (30), so no analytic input beyond convergence
−1
in |z| < λ is introduced. Part (d) is the orthogonality inversion for the
basis of irreducible characters on class functions, applied coefficient by coefficient. These four steps are precisely the determinant, primitive-channel,
and class-recovery assertions claimed as Main Theorem I.
□

The next lemma is a finite-matrix/Peter–Weyl translation of the twisted
gap into the skew-product peripheral spectrum. It is included as a background checkable criterion for the hypotheses used later, not as a new
dynamical extension criterion; compare the finite homogeneous extension
framework of [26, 27].

Therefore η < λ holds exactly when every non-trivial block Aϱ has spectral
radius strictly less than λ. Since every eigenvalue of Aϱ has modulus at most
ρ(Aϱ ), this is equivalent to saying that every eigenvalue of every non-trivial
block has modulus strictly less than λ, which is statement (ii). Because
the trivial block contributes exactly one peripheral eigenvalue, namely the
simple eigenvalue λ, statement (ii) is equivalent to saying that the peripheral
spectral multiset of Ae consists of that single copy of λ. This is statement

essential. Finally, statement (iii) says precisely that Ae has spectral radius λ,
that λ is algebraically simple, and that every other eigenvalue has modulus
strictly less than λ, which is statement (iv).

non-negative matrices says that irreducibility together with the absence of
any peripheral eigenvalue other than the simple Perron eigenvalue is equivalent to primitivity. Thus, under irreducibility, statements (i)–(iv) are also

Up to this point, the material is representation-theoretic infrastructure. The
specific content of the present paper begins when the determinant data are
pushed to the primitive level by Adams–Möbius inversion.
The next lemma fixes the trace convention used throughout the rest of
the section. It is the only place where the twisted matrices are identified
with fixed-point sums; afterwards all primitive formulas are obtained by
class-function expansion and Adams–Möbius inversion. Together with the
determinant linearisation and its primitive corollaries below, it forms Main
Theorem I from the introduction. The point of spelling it out separately
is that the passage from determinants to primitive data has two genuinely
distinct stages: matrix traces first give periodic Peter–Weyl sums, and only
then does Adams–Möbius inversion remove repetitions of primitive orbits.

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

Thus the identity in Lemma 3.6 is not a primitive-orbit statement; it is the
periodic input on which the primitive inversion operates.

Remark 3.9 (Where ordinary Möbius inversion stops). If ϕ = 1, the factor
ϕ(τ (γ)r ) in (16) is independent of r, and Corollary 3.10 reduces to the
usual scalar Möbius inversion for primitive orbit counts. For a non-trivial
class function this independence fails: the r-fold repetition changes the label
from g to g r . The Adams operator ψ r is exactly the bookkeeping needed
for this change. Thus the primitive finite-group step below is not obtained
by applying the scalar formula representation by representation; it uses the
Adams action on the character ring before Möbius inversion can remove
repetitions. For the general “primitive from repetitions” viewpoint, compare
the necklace/Witt formalism in [25, 48].

Remark 3.15 (Relation to necklace/Witt inversion and twisted-zeta precedents). The cancellation in Corollary 3.10 is the finite-group analogue of
the classical “primitive from repetitions” mechanism in the necklace and
Witt-vector literature [25, 48]. What is specific here is the finite-state cocycle input: repetitions replace a primitive label g by g m , so the relevant
correction is not scalar Möbius inversion alone but the Adams operation
ψ m ϕ(g) = ϕ(g m ) inside the character ring of one fixed finite-group extension.
Twisted periodic-point formulas and determinant identities also appear in
Fried’s work [13, 12] and in the dynamical-zeta treatment of Parry–Pollicott
[32]. Those works provide determinant and orbit-counting frameworks, but
they do not carry out the finite-state Adams–Möbius primitive inversion and
classwise Perron-point constant extraction developed here.

The determinant identity above is the generating-function part of Main Theorem I. The next theorem is the coefficient recovery from the same identity,
while the following corollary is only the data-interface version: the theorem
gives the formula, and the corollary records that the finite twisted trace, or
determinant, data is exactly the input needed to use it. Neither the twisted
spectral gap nor cyclic root-of-unity reconstruction enters this first main
theorem.
Remark 3.19 (Abelian toy model: the cyclic lift). Let G = Z/qZ, and let
χj (h) = ωqjh be its irreducible characters. The canonical cyclic-lift cocycle
is the special cocycle τq : EA → Z/qZ with τq (e) = 1 on every edge; its
skew-product adjacency matrix is A ⊗ Pq . For this cocycle, and not for a
general Z/qZ-valued edge labelling, one has Aχj = ωqj A, and therefore
Lχj (z) = log det(I − ωqj zA)−1 .
The Adams–Möbius inversion (17) then reduces to the ordinary Fourier decomposition on the q-th roots of unity. This is the concrete bridge between
the abelian formulas of Section 6 and the present finite-group formalism, but
only after the original cocycle has been replaced by this canonical cyclic-lift
cocycle. This model is also not an example of the twisted spectral gap used
later when q > 1: for every non-trivial character χj , ρ(Aχj ) = ρ(A) = λ,
so the non-trivial twisted radius is η = λ, not < λ. The cyclic-lift layer
is therefore a finite-matrix comparison case, not a source of Perron-point
non-trivial-channel constants.

Corollary 3.21 (Finite trace formula for primitive coefficients). The
coefficient-level primitive data are finite-state data. More precisely, the
(m)
finite character table of G, the Adams coefficients aϱ,π , and the twisted

Proof. The explicit coefficient formula is exactly Theorem 3.20. The coeffi(m)

This is the coefficient-level form of Main Theorem I, not a preliminary estimate: it is the point at which the finite twisted determinant family becomes
primitive orbit data. The trace lemma supplies the periodic input, the
linearisation theorem rewrites it as twisted determinant data, the Adams–
Möbius corollary removes repetitions, and the determinant corollary and
trace theorem give the primitive Peter–Weyl series and coefficients.
3.3. Bridge to classes and Main Theorem II. The primitive Peter–
Weyl coefficients determine the conjugacy-class counts by Fourier inversion
on G. The next proposition is a bridge: it translates the primitive channels
of Main Theorem I into class counts, but it does not add a new independent
main theorem.

This completes Main Theorem I in the sense used in the introduction: the

From this point onward in the Perron-point part of Main Theorem II we
assume λ > 1 and impose the gap hypothesis
η := max ρ(Aϱ ) < λ.

If G is trivial, this maximum is empty and η = 0. Thus the formal determinant identities Theorems 3.7, 3.20 and 3.23 and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18
are unconditional in the open Perron disc, while the estimates and constants
from Lemma 3.25 through Theorem 3.68 are conditional on λ > 1 and the
displayed gap. The only scalar input used below is the trivial-channel orbitMertens constant M(A); its finite-matrix formula is proved independently
in Section 6.
The class Mertens theorem below is the analytic part of Main Theorem II
from the introduction. The leading density term is included to identify the
constant term and normalisation; the new ingredient in this section is the
passage from the twisted determinant channels to the primitive Perron-point
class profile. The later Fourier formula is a recovery of the constants by
character orthogonality. The proof is assembled from the next five lemmas:
class decomposition, the non-trivial channel estimate, absolute convergence
at the Perron point, a geometric-harmonic tail bound, and the trivial Perronchannel contribution.

3.4. Class Mertens constants and Adams-corrected Euler products.
This subsection presents the product theorem and then its finite determinant
consequences. The first two subsubsections prove Main Theorems II and III:
additive class constants, then Adams-corrected class Euler products. The
determinant formula, stability statements, and obstruction certificates that
follow all concern the same constants. The proof route is:
class Mertens constants
↓ Euler product
class product constants
↓ fixed-label transform
finite determinant evaluation
↓ coordinate tests
stability and Artin obstruction.
Here Theorem 3.32 supplies the additive constants, while Theorems 3.11
and 3.38 prove Main Theorem III. The fixed-label definition, determinant
Perron formula, and KC -formula are tied by the finite coordinate equivalence below. The determinant formula and its finite truncation algorithm
are Propositions 3.44 and 3.56 and theorem 3.41; Propositions 3.58 and 3.60
and theorem 3.49 then separates the certified finite determinant arithmetic,
its S3 witness role, and the classical Frobenius-density contribution from the
Adams-corrected constant, and Proposition 3.47 and remark 3.48 record the
finite determinant dependence and recovery order for the three main theorems; the subsequent roadmap note explains the downstream status of the
product-constant refinements. These are synthesis statements: for hypotheses, branch conventions, and the classical-versus-new calibration one should
use the individual theorem packages cited in their statements and proofs.
The hyperplane and branch checks are Corollary 3.42 and lemmas 3.53
and 3.54; they verify that the same coordinate vector gives positive real
product constants with the stated normalisation. The obstruction statements are Corollaries 3.46 and 3.64 and theorem 3.63; they compare the
fixed-label transform with the periodic Artin logarithm after the product
constant has already been recovered. Thus the subsequent stability, obstruction, and quotient-shadow statements use the formula above without
introducing further product constants.
For reference, the finite-data closure statement below is placed at the
front because the section intentionally proves several consequences of the
same scalar-normalised vector rather than scattering them into independent
black boxes.
For dependency checking, the large proof spine should be read in the following order. First, Theorem 3.32 proves the additive Chebotarev–Mertens
asymptotic under the two headline hypotheses λ > 1 and η < λ. Second,
Theorems 3.11 and 3.38 converts those additive constants into the fixed-label
Euler-product constants; this is the only step where the distinction between
the fixed-label transform Fϱ and the periodic Artin logarithm Lϱ enters.
Third, Propositions 3.56 and 3.58, theorem 3.41, and corollary 3.43 evaluate the non-trivial Perron coordinates from the finite determinant data and
record the effective truncation convention. Finally, Theorems 3.49 and 3.63,

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

45

corollaries 3.45 and 3.64, and proposition 3.60 are comparison and obstruction consequences of the already recovered product vector. Thus the later
rigidity, stability, and S3 certificate statements do not feed back into the
proofs of Main Theorems II–III; they only test or separate the product constants produced by this ordered chain.
Proposition 3.31 (Finite-data closure for Main Theorems II–III). Assume
the standing hypotheses for the class-Mertens theorem, namely λ > 1 and
the strict twisted spectral gap η < λ. Let

More precisely:
(i) the first arrow is exactly Main Theorem I together with Theorem 3.32;
it uses coefficient extraction, Adams coefficients, finite character orthogonality, and only the regular Abel-path Perron boundary values of
the non-trivial twisted determinant series;
(ii) the second arrow is the class-Mertens identity (39);
(iii) the third arrow is the fixed-label Euler transform of Theorem 3.11;
(iv) the final equivalence is the scalar-normalised Fourier inversion (77) of
Theorem 3.49, equivalently the rigidity formula of Theorem 3.41.
Consequently the finite determinant data, together with the stated branch
and scalar-normalisation conventions, determines the whole output of Main
Theorems II–III. Conversely, once C(A) is fixed, equality of two productconstant vectors (KC )C is equivalent to equality of all non-trivial fixed-label
Perron coordinates (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 . Thus any attempted substitute for the
Adams-corrected product constants is correct for every conjugacy class if
and only if it matches the same non-trivial coordinate vector.
The source closure is literal. Let (A′ , τ ′ , G) be another finite-group extension satisfying the same hypotheses with the same Perron value λ, the same
scalar normalisation C(A′ ) = C(A), and the same group G. Suppose that
the branch-compatible determinant values which occur in Proposition 3.56

have identical values

Proof. Main Theorem I expresses the primitive Peter–Weyl coefficient
πn,ϱ (A, τ ) by Adams–Möbius inversion from the finite twisted determinant

evaluates the Perron-point primitive values Πϱ (λ−1 ) for ϱ ̸= 1 along the
specified Abel path and gives the class-Mertens constants by (39). This
proves the first two arrows.
The fixed-label Euler transform is a convergent Adams-weighted transform of the primitive channels at the Perron point; this is precisely Theorem 3.11. Substituting those values into Theorem 3.38 gives the product
constants. After the scalar base C(A) has been separated, the normalised

The final assertion follows immediately from the same inverse Fourier
formula. If a substitute has all the same class constants, its normalised
logarithmic vector is the same and therefore all non-trivial Fourier coordinates agree. If the coordinates agree, the displayed Fourier expansion gives
the same normalised logarithmic vector and hence the same class constants.
The stability, comparison, and obstruction results later in the subsection use
only these coordinates, so they factor through the stated chain.
It remains to prove the two-extension source-closure assertion. The
(m)
Adams coefficients aϱ,π , the Möbius factors, and the class characters
depend only on the common finite group G. Therefore the determinant
formula of Proposition 3.56 is the same absolutely convergent linear
combination of the displayed branch-compatible determinant values for the
two systems. Equality of those values gives Fϱ (λ−1 ) = Fϱ′ (λ−1 ) for every
ϱ ̸= 1. The same determinant-value hypothesis applies to the boundary
formula of Lemma 3.65, so Πϱ (λ−1 ) = Π′ϱ (λ−1 ) for every non-trivial ϱ. The
class-Mertens formula (39) then gives equality of the additive constants
MC , and the product formula (54), together with C(A′ ) = C(A), gives
equality of all KC .
Conversely, if the scalar normalisations and positive product vectors agree,
then the normalised logarithmic vectors K agree. Applying the inverse
Fourier formula (77), or equivalently (98), gives equality of every non-trivial

fixed-label coordinate. Thus an auxiliary record which leaves the scalar normalisation and these determinant boundary coordinates fixed is invisible to
the main constants, while changing a main constant necessarily changes one
of those two pieces of data. This proves the stated source closure.
□
Assembly of the three main statements. Under the hypotheses stated in the
introduction, the three displayed introduction theorems are exactly the following body theorem chain.
(i) Main Theorem I is the finite determinant interface of Proposition 3.1:
twisted traces give the class-function determinant logarithms, Adams–
Möbius inversion gives the primitive channels, and finite character
orthogonality gives the primitive Frobenius-class counts.
(ii) Main Theorem II is the Perron-boundary continuation of those same
primitive channels under λ > 1 and η < λ, namely Theorems 3.32
and 3.68 and proposition 3.66. It produces the class constants
MC (A, τ ; G) and their Hermitian Fourier–Parseval readout.
(iii) Main Theorem III is the fixed-label Euler-product evaluation and rigidity package Theorems 3.38, 3.41, 3.49 and 3.63, propositions 3.56
and 3.58, and corollaries 3.42, 3.43 and 3.46, with the finite S3 certificates used only as explicit witnesses to the Artin-substitution obstruction.
Consequently the introduction theorem environments are assembly statements: no additional hypothesis, boundary value, branch convention, scalar
normalisation, or appendix input is hidden in their restatement. In particular, the authoritative proof obligations are the body statements listed
above, and every output in Main Theorems I–III factors through the finitedata chain of Proposition 3.31.
For (i), Proposition 3.1 is proved before any Perron-boundary assumption is imposed. Its proof cites precisely Lemma 3.6, theorems 3.7, 3.20
and 3.23, and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18. These statements establish, in order, the fixed-point trace identity, the finite determinant linearisation of
Lϕ , the Adams–Möbius identity for Πϕ , the irreducible determinant-germ
formula, the primitive Peter–Weyl coefficient formula, and class-count recovery by finite character orthogonality. This is exactly the content of the
first introduction theorem.
For (ii), imposing λ > 1 and η < λ gives the non-trivial Perron-point values of the primitive channels by Theorem 3.32. The same theorem proves the
displayed class-Mertens asymptotic and the formula (39); Proposition 3.66
and theorem 3.68 then identify the normalised deviation profile and its Hermitian Fourier–Parseval transform. These are exactly the additional assertions made in the second introduction theorem, and their only new analytic
hypothesis is the strict finite-dimensional twisted gap already stated there.
For (iii), Theorem 3.38 proves the Frobenius-class Euler-product asymptotic with the fixed-label transform Fϱ (λ−1 ) and the product constant KC .
The determinant evaluation and finite certificate for those fixed-label Perron values are Propositions 3.56 and 3.58; substituting them into the class
product gives Theorem 3.41 and corollary 3.43. The comparison, stability,
scalar-baseline readout, and obstruction clauses are precisely Theorems 3.49

48

HAOBO MA

and 3.63 and corollaries 3.42 and 3.46. The cited S3 statements supply concrete finite witnesses to that last obstruction and do not add hypotheses
to the product theorem. Finally, Proposition 3.31 proves that these three
tiers are connected by one finite-data dependency chain, so the introduction
restatements introduce no independent theorem beyond the assembled body
chain.
□
Status note (Product-constant refinements). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.38, fix the scalar normalisation αA = γ + log C(A) and the non-trivial
Perron-boundary vector
F(A, τ ; G) := Fϱ (λ−1 ) ϱ̸=1 .

Then the hyperplane stability of Corollary 3.42, the assembled finite determinant formula and truncation of Corollary 3.43, the fixed-coordinate rank
obstruction of Corollary 3.45, and the Parseval obstruction budget of Corollary 3.64 are downstream refinements of the Main Theorem III coordinate
vector (αA , F(A, τ ; G)). More precisely, after the periodic Artin comparison
vector (Lϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 is fixed for the obstruction statements, each listed refinement is functorially determined by these already recovered coordinates.
Conversely, equality of the scalar-normalised fixed-coordinate product vector, or equality of the positive product constants with the same scalar normalisation, forces equality of F(A, τ ; G). Thus none of the listed refinements
supplies an independent main contribution, an additional hypothesis for the
product asymptotic, or a new source of class-product constants.
Indeed, Main Theorem III is Theorem 3.38. It gives

Lemma 3.35 (Exponentiating the class product logarithm). Let a ∈ R,
L ∈ R, and let RN = O(N −1 ) be a real sequence. If a positive sequence QN
satisfies
log QN = −a log N + L + RN ,
then

QN = eL N −a 1 + O(N −1 ) .
If the implied constant in RN = O(N −1 ) is uniform over a finite set of
indices, then the resulting multiplicative error is uniform over that set.
Proof. Choose B > 0 with |RN | ≤ B/N for all large N . Then
QN = eL N −a eRN .
For all large N , |RN | ≤ 1, and the elementary estimate |ex − 1| ≤ e|x| for
|x| ≤ 1 gives eRN = 1 + O(N −1 ). The same bound with the maximum of
the finitely many constants gives the uniform assertion.
□

Artin logarithm formula. Since ϕ is orthogonal to the trivial character and
η < λ, Theorem 3.11 gives absolute convergence of Fϕ (λ−1 ) and the determinant expression (50). In the expanded determinant expression the
coefficient of the single scalar boundary term is ⟨ϕ, 1⟩G = 0; hence that singular logarithm is omitted before the Abel limit is formed. The remaining
boundary terms have non-trivial twist and are regular by the strict twisted
gap, while every term with rm ≥ 2 lies strictly inside the Perron disc. This
is exactly the branch-compatible continuation used here. Finally, Proposition 3.12 proves that equality between Fϕ and Lϕ is equivalent to (23). Main

The exponent |C|/|G| and the existence of a Frobenius-class product
constant are the classical part of Theorem 3.38, parallel to the dynamical Mertens and Chebotarev product theorems of Sharp, Parry–Pollicott,

main chain is the branch-compatible Adams–Möbius determinant formula
for that constant, and hence the obstruction to replacing the fixed-label
Euler transform by an ordinary Artin logarithm.

chart; they are recorded for audit and reproducibility, not as additional main
theorems or as new hypotheses for the class-product asymptotic.

Proof. The determinant identity (58), including the local deletion of (1, 1, 1),
the Abel-boundary convention for non-trivial rm = 1 terms, the ordinary
interior evaluation for rm ≥ 2, and the absolute convergence of the remaining tail, is exactly Proposition 3.56. Hence the numbers Dϱ are fixed by the
stated finite determinant data.
The leading product exponent and the existence of the positive constants
are exactly Theorem 3.38; the historical calibration is the one stated in
Theorem 3.49. The character formula (54) expresses (KC )C in terms of
C(A) and the non-trivial values Fϱ (λ−1 ). Replacing those values by Dϱ
using (58), and then taking logarithms in the positive real branch fixed by
Theorem 3.38, gives the first identity in (61). Averaging this identity over
conjugacy classes with weights |C|/|G| gives the second identity in (60),
because every non-trivial irreducible character is orthogonal to the trivial
character. Multiplying the definition of HK by |C|/|G| and summing over
classes then gives the product constraint in (60). The inverse Fourier identity
in (61) and the Parseval identity (63) are the standard Hermitian character
orthogonality relations for the zero-mean class-function space.
Solving the definition (59) for KC and substituting the first identity in
(61) gives (62). This proves that the product vector is determined by the
listed scalar normalisation, character table, Adams tensors, branch tags, and
twisted determinant polynomials. Conversely, if the positive constants and
C(A) are known, then (59) constructs HK , and the inverse Hermitian Fourier
formula in (61) recovers each Dπ . The final equal-data assertion follows by
applying the same displayed formulae to the two systems. No step uses any
presentation, cover, fold, gauge, or cocycle-conjugacy invariant beyond these
fixed coordinates, which proves the asserted scope of the rigidity statement.
□

Proposition 3.44 (Branch-compatible fixed-coordinate equivalence for
product constants). Under the hypotheses and notation of Theorem 3.41,
consider two finite determinant-coordinate data sets with the same finite
group G, the same character table and Adams tensors, the same scalar normalisation C(A), and the same normalized Abel/ordinary branch convention
of Proposition 3.56. Thus equality of a coordinate always means equality
of the corresponding branch-compatible logarithmic determinant value,
not merely equality of an exponentiated determinant. If their non-trivial
fixed-label Perron vectors agree,
Fϱ (λ−1 ) = Fϱ′ (λ−1 )

(ϱ ̸= 1),

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

then they give the same Frobenius-class product constants KC = KC′ for
every conjugacy class C. Conversely, if the constants agree for every C and
the same scalar normalisation C(A) is used, then the non-trivial fixed-label
Perron vectors agree. Thus the recoverable invariant at Main Theorem III
is exactly the fixed branch-normalised determinant coordinate vector modulo
this finite Fourier equivalence, not a presentation-, cover-, gauge-, branchfree, or fold-invariant classification of the underlying cocycle.
Proof. With C(A) fixed, equality of the fixed-label Perron vectors makes
the right-hand side of (61) identical for every class. Substitution in the
exponential formula for KC in Theorem 3.41 therefore gives KC = KC′ for
all C. Conversely, equality of the positive constants and equality of C(A)
imply equality of the normalised logarithmic class functions HK defined in
(59). Applying the inverse character formula in (61) gives

exactly as in the fixed-matrix branch ambiguity recorded in Lemma A.5.
Thus no conclusion about alternative presentations, gauges, folds, branches,
or extensions is introduced.

Proof. First observe that dim HG = q − 1, because the real vector space of

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

65

Now let R : HG → W have the stated determining property. If V ∈ ker R,
then R(V ) = R(0), so the determining property gives Kα (V ) = Kα (0). By
injectivity of Kα , V = 0. Thus R is injective, and its rank is at least
dim HG = q − 1, proving (73).
Conversely, if a fixed-coordinate linear record is not injective, choose a
non-zero V ∈ ker R and put H = 0, H ′ = V . Then R(H) = R(H ′ ), but
the injectivity of Kα , already proved above, gives Kα (H) ̸= Kα (H ′ ). This is
the advertised bad pair and shows that the rank lower bound is exactly the
obstruction to determining all scalar-normalised product constants in the
fixed determinant-coordinate chart.
The finite character orthogonality relations identify the non-trivial irreducible characters with an orthonormal basis of the zero-mean class-function
space, with the standard conjugation symmetry when the original class function is real. Therefore the Peter–Weyl record (74) is an isometric injection
from HG onto a real coordinate space of dimension q − 1, so it attains the
lower bound.
For the dynamical constants, Theorem 3.41 identifies the coordinate tuple
of HK with (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 , and Proposition 3.56 evaluates those coordinates
from the finite twisted determinant family. This gives the claimed ranksharp fixed-coordinate interpretation of the determinant data. Finally, EB
is the zero-mean class function whose non-trivial Fourier coordinates are
Fϱ (λ−1 ) − Bϱ . Applying Parseval in the same zero-mean Peter–Weyl space
proves (75). If the right-hand side is non-zero, some classwise error is nonzero, so the substitute boundary cannot give all scalar-normalised product
constants; scalar data live in the trivial one-dimensional summand and have
already been fixed by α.

Proposition 3.47 (Finite determinant dependence of the main constants).
Assume λ > 1 and the twisted spectral gap η < λ of Theorem 3.32. Encode the finite group by its multiplication table and let the finite determinant
datum be


D(A, τ ; G) := G, {C ⊂ G conjugacy class}, (C, m) 7→ C m ,


C(A), {det(I − zAπ ) : π ∈ Irr(G)} .
Together with the Abel-path branch convention fixed separately in Theorem 3.32 and proposition 3.56, this finite datum determines the following
three nested output tiers. Each tier is asserted only under the hypotheses
and branch conventions of the theorem package cited for that tier.
(i) Algebraic determinant recovery: every primitive Peter–Weyl coefficient πn,ϱ (A, τ ) and hence every primitive class count pn,C ;
(ii) Perron-boundary recovery under the twisted gap: every non-trivial
Perron-point primitive value Πϱ (λ−1 ) and every class-Mertens constant MC (A, τ ; G);
(iii) Fixed-label product recovery: every fixed-label value Fϱ (λ−1 ) and every Frobenius-class Euler-product constant KC .
The recovery in item (i) is purely algebraic: coefficient extraction from the
finitely many determinant polynomials, finite character-table linear algebra,
Adams coefficients, Möbius inversion, and finite Fourier inversion. The
recovery in items (ii) and (iii) is the branch-compatible Perron-boundary
evaluation of those same finite determinant polynomials under η < λ, not
a claim that the boundary constants are finite algebraic expressions in the
polynomial coefficients alone. The singular scalar determinant logarithm
is never evaluated; the scalar normalisation uses the positive Perron constant C(A). Conversely, after the scalar normalisation C(A), the productconstant vector (KC )C determines exactly the non-trivial fixed-label Perron
vector (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 by the inverse Fourier formula in (77). Hence two extensions with identical data D(A, τ ; G), the same branch choices, and the
same scalar normalisation C(A) have identical outputs in Main Theorems I–
III. This proposition is only the closure of the assembled chain: the raw
determinant recovery is Corollary 3.18 and theorem 3.23, the Perron-point

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

67

convergence and additive constants are Theorems 3.32 and 3.68, and the
product-level fixed-label constants are Theorems 3.38 and 3.41 and proposition 3.56.
Proof. For tier (i), the finite group component of D(A, τ ; G) gives the complete character table, the irreducible characters, conjugacy classes, and the
Adams class maps C 7→ C m . The determinant polynomials
determine all
P
traces Tr(Anπ ) by the finite identity log det(I − zAπ )−1 = n≥1 Tr(Anπ )z n /n
on its formal power-series side. Lemma 3.6 identifies these traces with the
periodic twisted fixed-point sums. Finite character orthogonality then gives
Theorem 3.7, and the Adams–Möbius formula in Corollary 3.10 converts
the periodic class-function coefficients to primitive coefficients. Therefore
Corollaries 3.18 and 3.21 and theorem 3.23 recover all primitive Peter–Weyl
coefficients and primitive class counts from D(A, τ ; G).
For tier (ii), under η < λ, the non-trivial determinant series are regular at the Perron point along the branch specified in Theorem 3.32. That
theorem, together with Proposition 3.66 and theorem 3.68, evaluates the
non-trivial primitive values Πϱ (λ−1 ) and hence the class-Mertens constants.
For tier (iii), the fixed-label Euler-transform theorem Theorem 3.38 and the
finite determinant formula Proposition 3.56 evaluate Fϱ (λ−1 ) for every nontrivial ϱ; substituting these values into Theorem 3.41 gives the Frobeniusclass product constants KC . The scalar singular determinant is not evaluated in these steps: the scalar contribution is the supplied Perron constant C(A), exactly as in the displayed product formulas of Theorems 3.38
and 3.41. Thus the proof uses exactly the branch conventions and convergence hypotheses attached to each tier’s theorem package.
Finally, the inverse formula (77) is a finite Fourier inversion on the class
algebra after the scalar baseline has been fixed. It shows that the vector
(KC )C determines precisely the non-trivial vector (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 , and the direct product formula gives the converse dependence. Every operation used
above is therefore either finite coefficient extraction, finite character-table
algebra, Adams–Möbius inversion, or the already proved branch-compatible
Perron-point evaluation under η < λ. The proposition adds no new leading
asymptotic claim beyond the cited packages: the Frobenius-density exponent in the product theorem is classical, while the new finite-data content
used here is the Adams–Möbius fixed-label determinant recovery of the constants. Identical finite collections with identical branch choices and scalar
normalisation pass through the same sequence of operations, so they have
identical outputs in the three main theorems.
□
Remark 3.48 (Ordered finite recovery). Under the hypotheses of Proposition 3.47, the finite-data recovery factors through the following ordered
chain:
(76)

D(A, τ ; G) −→ OI −→ OII −→ OIII ,

where OI consists of the primitive Peter–Weyl coefficients and primitive class
counts, OII consists of the non-trivial Perron-point primitive values and
class-Mertens constants, and OIII consists of the fixed-label Perron values
and Frobenius-class product constants. The first arrow uses only coefficient

68

HAOBO MA

extraction, Adams coefficients, and finite Fourier inversion. The second arrow is the Abel-branch Perron-boundary evaluation supplied by the twisted
spectral gap. The third arrow is the fixed-label Euler-transform and classproduct formula of Theorem 3.38 and Theorem 3.41. Thus Proposition 3.47
and corollary 3.43 are finite recoveries of Main Theorems I–III, while Theorem 3.41 is the branch-compatible fixed-coordinate rigidity theorem for the
Adams-corrected product vector after scalar normalisation. In particular,
equality of two finite determinant data implies equality of all three ordered
outputs, and after C(A) is fixed equality of the product-constant vectors is
equivalent to equality of the non-trivial fixed-label Perron vectors.
The factor N −|C|/|G| and the existence of a Frobenius-class Mertens product asymptotic are the classical part of (52); compare the Frobenius-class
product theorem of Mesliza–Noorani [24], Sharp’s dynamical Mertens theorem [41], and the Chebotarev orbit-distribution antecedents [31, 27]. Nearby
finite-data and skew-product works such as [9, 28] address different rigidity
or extension questions. The finite-state primitive-label contribution needed
here is the following named comparison theorem: it is the Adams correction
to the Frobenius-class Mertens product, not merely a restatement of the
classical density theorem.

Applying Parseval to the class function EB gives the second identity. The

and otherwise the normalised error is non-zero for at least one conjugacy

□
Thus Main Theorems I–III are finite-state determinant-to-primitive,
Perron-point, and Adams-corrected product-constant theorems. They do
not assert a new geometric periodic-orbit invariant, a Ruelle-resonance
theorem, a finite-data rigidity theorem, a general certificate format, or
an operator-algebraic skew-product classification. The shadow results
Theorems 5.4, 5.8 and 5.10, the eta-gap tests Theorem 4.1, corollary 4.2,
and proposition 4.6, and the S3 certificates Theorem 5.14, propositions 5.15
and C.4, and corollary 5.16 are internal finite-state tools for the same
product-coordinate picture, not comparisons with the cited geometric, resonance, finite-data-rigidity, computational-certificate, or operator-algebraic
papers.
Proposition 3.50 (Theorem-by-theorem comparison calibration). Under
the hypotheses of Theorem 3.32, the logical role of the cited comparison literature in Main Theorems I–III is exactly the following.
(a) Main Theorem I, Theorems 3.7 and 3.20 and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18,
is calibrated against Parry–Pollicott’s Chebotarev theorem for finite Galois coverings [31]: the comparison layer is orbit distribution and Artin–
Ruelle motivation, while the contribution proved here is finite characteralgebra inversion from periodic twisted determinants to primitive Peter–
Weyl channels.
(b) The same Main Theorem I package is calibrated separately against
Noorani–Parry’s Chebotarev theorem for shifts of finite type [27]: the
comparison layer is SFT Frobenius-class equidistribution, while the
contribution proved here is Adams–Möbius recovery of primitive Peter–
Weyl channels without invoking an additional Tauberian orbit-counting
theorem.
(c) Main Theorem II, Theorems 3.32 and 3.68 and proposition 3.66, is
calibrated against Sharp’s orbit-Mertens theorem [41]: the comparison
layer is the classical logarithmic scale, while the contribution proved here
is the finite-state determinant formula for the class-indexed constant
term under the stated Perron gap.
(d) Main Theorem II and the product normalisation theorem Theorems 3.32
and 3.38 are calibrated against Mesliza–Noorani’s Frobenius-class
Mertens theorem [24]: the comparison layer is product existence and
scale, while the contribution proved here is explicit finite Peter–Weyl
boundary evaluation of the classwise constants.
(e) Main Theorem III, Theorems 3.38, 3.41, 3.49 and 3.63 and proposition 3.56, is calibrated against ordinary Artin–Ruelle determinant analogues in the symbolic-dynamical lineage [32]: the contribution proved
here is the Adams-corrected fixed-label Euler value Fϱ (λ−1 ), its determinant recovery, and the obstruction to replacing it by Lϱ (λ−1 ).

72

HAOBO MA

(f) The quotient/shadow readouts Theorems 5.4 and 5.8 are calibrated
against Coles’s Vassiliev-writhe/Axiom A invariant setting [8]: no geometric periodic-orbit invariant is asserted here; the retained statements
are finite SFT cocycle determinant-coordinate readouts.
(g) The eta-gap package Theorem 4.1, corollary 4.2, and proposition 4.6 is
calibrated against Humbert’s first-Ruelle-resonance comparison setting
[17]: no Banach-space or resonance theorem is asserted here; the retained assertions are finite-matrix spectral-gap tests at the Perron point.
(h) The cyclic-detection and finite-state certificate boundary Theorem 5.10
and proposition 4.6 is calibrated against O’Hare’s finite-data rigidity
theorem [28]: no finite-data rigidity theorem is asserted here; the paper
gives explicit finite-state readouts and detection boundaries instead.
(i) The non-abelian obstruction package Theorem 5.14, propositions 5.15
and C.4, and corollary 5.16 is calibrated against Crismale–Del Vecchio–
Griseta–Rossi’s non-commutative skew-product framework [9]: no
operator-algebraic skew-product classification is asserted here; the
retained results are explicit finite-group character obstructions for the
fixed SFT cocycle model.
Consequently Main Theorems I–III depend on the classical comparison
sources only through the explicitly cited symbolic-dynamical orbit-counting,
Mertens, and determinant framework, while the recent 2025 comparison
papers are not proof inputs for any implication in the main theorem chain.
Proof. For Main Theorem I, the asserted dependency is exactly the finite
determinant interface in Proposition 3.1 together with the class-function linearisation and Adams–Möbius inversion in Theorem 3.7 and corollary 3.10.
Those results start from the finite matrices Aϱ and the character identities of the finite group; their proofs use the determinant expansions and
Adams operations displayed there, not an additional orbit-counting theorem. Thus Parry–Pollicott’s finite-covering Chebotarev theorem supplies the
classical Artin–Ruelle orbit-distribution model, Noorani–Parry supplies the
corresponding shift-of-finite-type Chebotarev comparison, and the primitivechannel inversion is the internal finite-label step.
For Main Theorem II, Theorem 3.32 assumes the strict non-trivial spectral
gap and isolates the leading term (|C|/|G|) log N . The constant term is then
evaluated by the finite Peter–Weyl formula and the Perron-boundary values
in Theorem 3.68. This proves the Sharp row: Sharp supplies the relevant
closed-orbit Mertens scale, but the determinant expression for MC (A, τ ; G)
is the internal output. The same theorem and Theorem 3.38 give the
Mesliza–Noorani row: their Frobenius-class Mertens comparison fixes the
product scale and existence layer, whereas the finite Peter–Weyl Perronboundary evaluation is proved here from the twisted finite matrices.
For Main Theorem III, Theorem 3.38 first converts the logarithmic
Main Theorem II estimate and the absolutely convergent product tail
into PC (N ) = KC N −|C|/|G| (1 + O(N −1 )). The sentence in that theorem
separates the classical exponent and existence layer from the local constant
calculation. The constant is then rewritten through Fϱ (λ−1 ), and Proposition 3.56 and theorems 3.41, 3.49 and 3.63 identify its determinant value
and the Artin-replacement obstruction. Hence the third row follows.

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

73

Finally, the modern comparison papers cited in the last four rows are
separated source by source. Coles concerns geometric periodic-orbit observables, while Theorems 5.4 and 5.8 use only finite quotient and character
maps. Humbert concerns Ruelle-resonance behaviour, while the eta-gap
package uses only spectra of finite twisted adjacency matrices. O’Hare
concerns finite-data rigidity, while the cyclic-detection and certificate statements here give explicit readouts and boundaries rather than a rigidity theorem. Crismale–Del Vecchio–Griseta–Rossi concern non-commutative skewproduct structure, while the S3 package uses finite character tables and determinant coordinates. None of these 2025 results appears in the hypotheses
or proofs of the theorem packages just listed. This proves both the venue
calibration and the asserted non-dependence of the Main Theorems on those
recent comparison sources.
□

Remark 3.57 (Convention audit for Main Theorem III). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.38, the determinant form of (54) has the following forced
conventions.
(i) The sign of KC is the single outer minus sign in (54); equivalently KC
is the positive real exponential of the negative logarithmic constant
in (80).
(ii) The class coefficient is (|C|/|G|)χ∗ϱ (C), where χ∗ϱ (C) = χϱ (C); inverse recovery of Fourier coordinates uses χσ (C) on the summing
side, not a second conjugated coefficient.
(iii) In the determinant expansion of each Fϱ (λ−1 ), the single scalar
boundary triple (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1) is omitted before evaluation and
is not replaced by a finite part.
(iv) Non-trivial rm = 1 determinant logarithms are Abel-path boundary
values of the normalized germ at the origin, while all rm ≥ 2 terms
are ordinary interior-disc values of the same germ.
(v) The determinant double tail is absolutely convergent at the Perron
boundary after this local omission, so the order of the finite character sums and the Abel limit used in (54) is justified by dominated
convergence.
Proof. Item (i) is exactly
Lemma 3.54, which derives the sign from the idenP
tity log(1 − x) = − r≥1 xr /r and from the scalar normalization M(A) +
P
−r
r≥2 Π1 (λ )/r = γ + log C(A). Item (ii) is Lemma 3.52 together with the
Fourier convention in Section 3: class indicators expand with the forward coefficient χ∗ϱ (C), and orthogonality recovers the σ-coordinate by multiplying
by χσ (C) and summing over conjugacy classes.
Items (iii) and (iv) are the content of Lemma 3.55: the coefficient of the

determinant values from Proposition 3.56 into (54) gives precisely the determinant form of Main Theorem III, with no remaining sign, conjugation,
branch, deletion, or convergence ambiguity.

is at most ϵ/2, and choosing M so that the analytic tail is at most ϵ/2,
gives a rigorous output interval of radius at most ϵ. Thus the input, branch
convention, finite output, and error bound are all finite and explicit.
