Appendix-level reproducibility records. The local rational reproducibility
record and five-field S3 audit format are placed in Proposition E.1 and corollary E.3. They are appendix-level audit formats for the finite determinant
computability theorem Proposition 3.58; the main proof path uses only the
determinant formula, the finite truncation bound, and finite character-table
Fourier inversion.
Product-level finite consequence. The finite reproducibility record of Proposition 3.58 is finite mathematical support for the determinant-coordinate
values used in Main Theorem III. Fix a non-trivial irreducible character ϱ,
a truncation window M , rational branch rectangles for every non-deleted
(m)
triple (r, m, π) with rm ≤ M , the Adams coefficients aϱ,π , and rational
radii RM , Rnum as in Proposition 3.58. If these finite records pass the exact
character-table, branch-tag, interval-propagation, and tail-bound checks of
that proposition, then they certify the explicit enclosure
Fϱ (λ−1 ) ∈ IM + {w ∈ C : |w| ≤ RM + Rnum },
where IM is the rational interval obtained from the finite determinant sum.
Applying this finite certificate for all ϱ ̸= 1 and then applying the finite
Fourier inverse of Corollary 3.59 certifies rational enclosures for the logarithms of all class-product constants KC . Thus the displayed finite records
prove the following four mathematical assertions and no broader certifiedcomputation theorem.
(i) The accepted finite record for ϱ determines a rigorous rational enclosure for the single fixed-label Perron value Fϱ (λ−1 ), with the scalar
Perron logarithm deleted before any boundary value is taken.
(ii) The collection of accepted records over ϱ ̸= 1 determines, by finite
Fourier inversion and the fixed scalar normalisation C(A), rigorous
enclosures for the class-product constants of Theorem 3.38.
(iii) The S3 appendix records instantiate this finite certificate as a witness
to the non-abelian Artin-substitution obstruction; they add no new
analytic hypothesis to Main Theorem III.
(iv) Nothing in the finite certificate asserts a general-purpose certified computation package for arbitrary finite groups, arbitrary symbolic matrices, arbitrary numerical branch tracking, or arbitrary determinant
identities beyond the determinant formula proved in this article.

82

HAOBO MA

Proof. The displayed enclosure is exactly (176) from Proposition 3.58. That
proposition proves that the four finite records enclose the truncated determinant sum and its discarded tail, and it also proves that the omitted scalar
Perron triple has coefficient zero for ϱ ̸= 1. Thus (i) is a finite consequence
of the determinant formula in Proposition 3.56; no limiting orbit count, unrecorded branch choice, or numerical assumption is introduced.
Applying the same accepted-record argument to every non-trivial irreducible character gives enclosures for the full non-trivial vector (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 .
The inverse and forward transforms in Corollary 3.59 are finite charactertable operations after C(A) has fixed the scalar coordinate. Therefore those
enclosures transport to the logarithms of the class-product constants in Theorem 3.38, proving (ii).
Statement (iii) follows from Corollary E.3: the appendix files are local finite arithmetic transcripts for the same five fields. The concrete S3 obstruction in Proposition C.4 and lemmas C.5 and D.2 uses those transcripts only
after Main Theorem III has supplied the analytic determinant-coordinate
formula. Hence the witness illustrates and certifies the obstruction but does
not enlarge the theorem hypotheses.
For (iv), inspect the inputs permitted in Proposition 3.58: the group, character table, power maps, determinant polynomials, Perron root, strict-gap
certificate, branch convention, majorant, truncation window, and rational
interval bounds are all fixed finite records. The proof of Proposition 3.58
checks only algebraic equalities, rational inequalities, interval containments,
and the tail bound already derived from Lemma 3.14. These checks are
sufficient for the finite records used here, but they do not construct a general parser, branch tracker, interval-arithmetic library, or universal certification theory for all finite groups and matrices. Thus the result is a finite
determinant certificate for the constants in this paper, not a standalone
certified-computation framework.

Proof. The asserted non-interference is a limit comparison. The formulas

already proved in Theorems 3.32 and 3.38. In the refined summatory identity (102) the auxiliary contribution is the single compensated tail −TC (N ).

Perron values.

Remark 3.62 (Comparison with classical Mertens and Chebotarev asymptotics). The conclusion of Theorem 3.32 should be read as a finite-state
classwise constant formula under an already stated twisted spectral gap. It
does not supply a new Tauberian proof of a Mertens theorem of the type
proved by Sharp [41], nor a new leading-density Chebotarev theorem of the
type proved for Axiom A or homogeneous extensions in [31, 27]. Its extra
output is the explicit determinant expression for the class-indexed constant
|C|
log N behaviour has already been
term MC (A, τ ; G) after the leading |G|
separated. In this sense MC (A, τ ; G) is a classwise finite-state analogue of
the constant term in classical orbit-counting asymptotics, not a new Tauberian theorem of Sharp type. The theorem-by-theorem calibration against
the classical and recent comparison results is recorded in Table 1.

Status note (Obstruction refinements). Under the hypotheses of Theorem 3.38, the core Main Theorem III data consist of the Frobenius-class
product asymptotic, the fixed-label constants KC , the finite determinant
evaluation of Fϱ (λ−1 ), and the Adams–Möbius obstruction to the naive
Artin substitution. The fixed-coordinate rank obstruction of Corollary 3.45
and the Parseval bound of Corollary 3.64 are determined functorially by
those data together with the already defined periodic Artin logarithms:

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

89

if two finite-state extensions have the same scalar baseline γ + log C(A),
the same non-trivial fixed-label boundary values Fϱ (λ−1 ), and the same
periodic Artin logarithms Lϱ (λ−1 ), then they have the same scalarnormalised fixed-coordinate product vector, the same class constants, and
the same Artin-substitution obstruction bound. Conversely, equality of
the scalar-normalised fixed-coordinate product vector forces equality of
all non-trivial fixed-label boundary values. Hence the fixed-coordinate
rank obstruction and the Parseval obstruction bound are downstream
corollary-level refinements of Main Theorem III, not independent main
theorems and not additional hypotheses for the product asymptotic.
Main Theorem III, in the form of Theorem 3.38, gives the constants


KC = exp−

Thus the scalar baseline together with the tuple (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 determines
every KC , and Corollary 3.45 then identifies this tuple with the unique zeromean Peter–Weyl coordinate vector of the scalar-normalised product constants. The same corollary gives the converse: equality of scalar-normalised
fixed-coordinate product vectors implies equality of their non-trivial Peter–
Weyl coordinates, hence equality of all values Fϱ (λ−1 ).
The Artin-substitution constants are obtained from the same displayed
formula after replacing each fixed-label value by Lϱ (λ−1 ). Therefore the
difference vector Dϱ = Fϱ (λ−1 ) − Lϱ (λ−1 ), the zero-mean classwise error
E(C), and the norm identity in Corollary 3.64 are all functions of the already fixed Main Theorem III data and of the corresponding periodic Artin
logarithms. If the periodic Artin logarithms also agree, then the difference
vector agrees, and so the Parseval bound agrees. No additional asymptotic
input, determinant channel, or Perron-point hypothesis is introduced.

Remark 3.67 (On the twisted spectral-gap hypothesis). The condition
η = max ρ(Aϱ ) < λ
ϱ̸=1

does not enter the determinant identities themselves: the formulas of Theorem 3.7 and corollaries 3.10 and 3.18 are valid throughout the disc |z| < λ−1
without it. The role of the hypothesis is exactly to permit evaluation at the
Perron point z = λ−1 in the non-trivial channels and to convert the primitive Peter–Weyl series into the class-indexed constants MC (A, τ ; G). From
the dynamical point of view this is the representation-by-representation analogue of separating the Perron eigenvalue from the remaining spectrum.
The leading Chebotarev and Frobenius-class Mertens phenomena for finite homogeneous extensions belong to the existing orbit-counting lineage,
beginning here with Noorani–Parry [27] and Noorani’s Markov-shift extension criteria [26], and continuing through the Axiom A, Livšic, skew-product
reciprocity, and homology-class settings of [31, 30, 33, 34, 36, 18, 42, 35, 7].
The present paper uses the gap as a stated finite-dimensional hypothesis,
not as a new structural criterion for cocycles. In the finite-state setting
it is already checkable: Lemma 3.4 identifies it with the absence of none equivalently
trivial peripheral spectrum in the skew-product adjacency A,
with Ae having a simple strictly dominant Perron eigenvalue, and, when Ae is
e What remains outside the present arguirreducible, with primitivity of A.
ment is a structural criterion on τ that would imply this condition without
first testing the full skew-product matrix. The new step here is instead
the finite-state Adams–Möbius calculus at the level of primitive constants
and the explicit class-indexed determinant formula MC (A, τ ; G), not a new
leading-density theorem of the type considered in [20, 8].
3.5. Hermitian Fourier readout of class constants. The class-indexed
constants MC (A, τ ; G) form the readout interface between Main Theorems II and III. Main Theorem II first produces their deviations from the
trivial average as Perron-point primitive channels, using the Adams–Möbius
determinant formula and the strict twisted gap. The present subsection
then applies Hermitian character orthogonality to read those channels back
from the class constants. This is the final step in Main Theorem II and
the vector of Perron-point values that Main Theorem III inserts into the
fixed-label Euler product; it is not a cyclic one-layer sampling argument.

Together with Theorem 3.32 and Proposition 3.66, this proves Main Theorem II from the introduction. The determinant and Adams–Möbius work
has already produced the Perron-point primitive channels; the final step
above is the standard character-orthogonality recovery on class functions,
not the one-layer DFT calculation used later for the canonical cyclic-lift
comparison.

93

Remark 3.69 (recovery analogy stops at the orthogonality step). Theorem 3.68 has the same formal recovery shape as the root-of-unity
reconstruction results from Section 6. In the separate canonical cyclic-lift
comparison case, one root-of-unity layer recovers the reduced spectral
data by ordinary Fourier inversion. Here the full family of conjugacy-class
Mertens constants MC (A, τ ; G) recovers the non-trivial twisted primitive
values Πϱ (λ−1 ) by non-abelian character orthogonality. The analogy is only
at the final recovery step: the class-indexed constants being inverted here
have first been produced by the Adams–Möbius determinant mechanism
and the twisted gap, whereas the one-layer statement of Section 6 is
polynomial coefficient recovery.
4. Diagnostic Boundary for the Twisted Spectral Gap
The strict inequality η = maxϱ̸=1 ρ(Aϱ ) < λ is the input that permits
evaluation of non-trivial Peter–Weyl channels at the Perron point; if G is
trivial, this empty maximum is 0. This section is only a finite-state diagnostic for the boundary case η = λ, not a second source of class-product
constants and not a general sufficient-condition theory for the strict gap.
The boundary case is rigid: equality is equivalent to the existence of a
cocycle-equivariant line field. The corollaries below are therefore used only
as operational checks for the hypothesis already stated in the main theorems: they classify the one-dimensional loop-character obstruction at the
boundary and record the perfect-loop-group consequence, but they do not
attempt to classify all strict-gap cocycles.

Corollary 4.2 (Finite-state diagnostic for the strict-gap input). This is
the operational test for the gap hypothesis. It contains no product-constant
formula; it only decides whether the finite extension has a non-trivial Perronboundary channel. Thus the diagnostic criterion below is used only to certify
the hypothesis of Main Theorems II–III before the Adams–Möbius determinant recovery is applied. Suppose G is non-trivial. Then the following are
equivalent.
(1) The Perron-point input used in the class-product theorem fails,
namely η = λ.
(2) There exist a non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ ∈ Irr(G), a
phase ω ∈ T, and unit vectors ξi ∈ Vϱ , one for each vertex, such
that
(119)

ϱ(τ (e))ξj = ωξi

for every admissible edge e : i → j.

Consequently, for this fixed finite-state cocycle, η < λ holds exactly when
the finite list of edge equations (119) has no solution in any non-trivial irreducible representation. In this case the hypotheses of Theorem 3.38 are
reduced to the verified absence of these boundary line fields.
Proof. By Proposition 3.5, every non-trivial channel satisfies ρ(Aϱ ) ≤ λ.
Since G is finite, there are only finitely many irreducible representations, so
the maximum defining η is achieved. Hence η = λ if and only if ρ(Aϱ ) = λ

96

HAOBO MA

for at least one non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G). Applying Theorem 4.1 to that representation gives exactly the edge equations (119), and the converse implication in the same theorem shows that any solution of those equations forces
ρ(Aϱ ) = λ, hence η = λ. The final sentence is the contrapositive, together
with the role of the strict-gap hypothesis stated in Theorem 3.38.
□
Corollary 4.3 (Loop-character classification of boundary channels). Fix a
non-trivial irreducible unitary representation ϱ : G → U (Vϱ ). For a phase
ω ∈ T and a vertex i, let
Wi (ϱ, ω) :=

\

ker ϱ(τ (γ)) − ω |γ| I ,


γ:i→i

where the intersection is over all closed admissible paths based at i. Then
ρ(Aϱ ) = λ if and only if there are a phase ω ∈ T and unit vectors ξi ∈
Wi (ϱ, ω), one for each vertex, such that
ϱ(τ (e))ξj = ωξi

(e : i → j).

For every such solution, the line Li := Cξi is a one-dimensional Γi (τ )subrepresentation of ϱ|Γi (τ ) , with character χi : Γi (τ ) → T determined by
χi (τ (γ)) = ω |γ|

(γ : i → i).

Moreover, if p : i → j is any admissible path of length |p|, then ω −|p| ϱ(τ (p))
gives a unitary similarity Lj → Li . Thus the Perron-boundary obstruction
is precisely a compatible system of one-dimensional loop characters and edge
similarities.
Proof. If ρ(Aϱ ) = λ, then Theorem 4.1 supplies ω and unit vectors ξi satisfying the displayed edge equations. Iterating those equations around a closed
path γ : i → i gives
ϱ(τ (γ))ξi = ω |γ| ξi ,
so ξi ∈ Wi (ϱ, ω) for every i. Conversely, if such unit vectors in the spaces
Wi (ϱ, ω) satisfy the edge equations, the converse half of Theorem 4.1 gives
ρ(Aϱ ) = λ.
It remains only to identify the character data. The closed-path identity
just displayed says that every generator τ (γ) of Γi (τ ) preserves Li and acts
on it by the scalar ω |γ| . Products and inverses of such generators therefore
also preserve Li , and the scalar by which an element of Γi (τ ) acts on the
one-dimensional space Li defines a well-defined group homomorphism χi :
Γi (τ ) → T. This homomorphism has the stated value on every closed-path
label. Finally, iterating the edge equations along a path p : i → j gives
ϱ(τ (p))ξj = ω |p| ξi ; hence ω −|p| ϱ(τ (p)) maps the unit line Lj unitarily onto
Li . This proves the announced classification.
□
Proposition 4.4 (Loop-subgroup obstruction to Perron-boundary channels). Suppose G is non-trivial, and fix a vertex i0 . Put H := Γi0 (τ ) and
H ′ := [H, H]. If the Perron-boundary obstruction η = λ occurs, then there
is a non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ ∈ Irr(G) such that
′

(Vϱ )H := {v ∈ Vϱ : ϱ(h)v = v for every h ∈ H ′ }
is non-zero. Equivalently, if for some vertex i0 every non-trivial irreducible
representation ϱ of G satisfies (Vϱ )[Γi0 (τ ),Γi0 (τ )] = 0, then η < λ. In that case

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

97

the strict twisted spectral-gap hypothesis required in Main Theorems II–III,
and hence in Theorems 3.32 and 3.38, is certified by this single loop-subgroup
test.
Proof. Assume first that η = λ. By Corollary 4.2, there are a non-trivial
irreducible representation ϱ, a phase ω ∈ T, and a boundary line field (ξi )i .
Applying Corollary 4.3 at the fixed vertex i0 , the line Li0 := Cξi0 is invariant
under H = Γi0 (τ ), and the action of H on that line is by a one-dimensional
character χ : H → T. Since T is abelian, χ is trivial on the commutator
subgroup H ′ = [H, H]. Hence every element of H ′ fixes the non-zero vector
′
ξi0 , so ξi0 ∈ (Vϱ )H and the displayed fixed-vector space is non-zero.
The equivalent obstruction statement is the contrapositive of the first
assertion, together with the finite-state diagnostic Corollary 4.2: if the fixedvector space above vanishes for every non-trivial irreducible ϱ, then the
boundary case η = λ cannot occur. Since Proposition 3.5 gives ρ(Aϱ ) ≤ λ
in every non-trivial channel and the maximum defining η is finite, exclusion
of equality is exactly η < λ. The final sentence records the role of this
certified strict gap in the two Perron-point product theorems cited in the
statement.
□
Corollary 4.5 (Perfect loop groups force the eta gap). Suppose G is nontrivial. Assume that for some vertex i0 the loop-label subgroup Γi0 (τ ) is
perfect and that, for every non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ of G, the
restricted representation ϱ|Γi0 (τ ) has no non-zero fixed vector. Then η < λ.
In particular, if Γi0 (τ ) = G for some vertex i0 and G is perfect, then the
strict eta-gap hypothesis required in Main Theorems II–III holds.
Proof. Let H = Γi0 (τ ). Since H is perfect, [H, H] = H. The fixed-vector
hypothesis is therefore precisely the vanishing condition in Proposition 4.4,
and that proposition gives η < λ.
For the stated special case, let Γi0 (τ ) = G and assume that G is perfect.
If a non-trivial irreducible ϱ had a non-zero G-fixed vector, the span of that
vector would be a non-zero invariant subspace on which G acts trivially;
irreducibility would force ϱ itself to be the trivial representation. This is
impossible for ϱ ̸= 1. Thus the fixed-vector hypothesis in the first part is
automatic, and the strict gap follows.
□

item (i). In particular, the perfect-loop criterion of Corollary 4.5 is a structural route into the same finite-state certificate, not an additional productconstant formula. Under these certificates the constants MC (A, τ ; G) and

Proof. The equivalence of (i) and (ii) is exactly the skew-product peripheralspectrum criterion of Lemma 3.4: the Peter–Weyl block decomposition of Ae
e with algebraic multiplicities, as the disjoint union
gives the spectrum of A,
of the spectra of the blocks Aϱ , each repeated dim ϱ times. The trivial block

precisely when every non-trivial block has spectral radius strictly smaller
than λ.
The equivalence of (i) and (iii) is Corollary 4.2 when non-trivial irreducible
representations exist. If none exist, then G is trivial, η = 0 by convention,
and the finite line-field condition is vacuous, so both (i) and (iii) hold because
λ > 1. In the remaining case, Theorem 4.1 says that equality ρ(Aϱ ) = λ for a
fixed non-trivial irreducible representation is equivalent to the displayed linefield equations for that representation. Since G is finite, only finitely many
irreducible representations occur, and the maximum defining η is attained.
Therefore the strict gap fails exactly when one of these finite systems has a
solution.
The final assertion is a substitution of this certificate into the hypotheses
of Theorems 3.32 and 3.38. Those theorems require λ > 1 and η < λ for the
Perron-point evaluation of the non-trivial primitive Peter–Weyl channels;
the present proposition only identifies that hypothesis with finite matrix or
finite algebraic tests. The cited loop-subgroup obstruction proves a sufficient condition for item (i), and the perfect-loop criterion is its immediate
structural specialization. None of these diagnostics supplies an additional
constant formula when the tests fail.
□

(i) For every non-trivial irreducible representation ϱ, the branch

ϱ, a phase ω ∈ T, and a boundary line field satisfying (117). Moreover
ωλ ∈ spec(Aϱ ), so the non-trivial determinant factor det(I − zAϱ )−1 has a
pole at z = (ωλ)−1 on the Perron circle. Thus the finite-state certificate of
Proposition 4.6 is a necessary and sufficient verification gate for the Perronpoint constants in Main Theorems II–III; it is not an automatic existence
theorem for arbitrary cocycles.
Proof. The equivalence between η < λ and item (i) is purely finite dimensional. If η < λ, then every eigenvalue α of every non-trivial block Aϱ
satisfies |α| ≤ ρ(Aϱ ) < λ. Hence 1/α, for α ̸= 0, lies strictly outside the
Q
closed disc |z| ≤ λ−1 . The determinant det(I − zAϱ ) = α (1 − zα) therefore has no zero on a neighbourhood of that closed disc, and the logarithm
branch normalized at 0 extends holomorphically there.
Conversely, if item (i) holds and some non-trivial Aϱ had ρ(Aϱ ) = λ, then
an eigenvalue α ∈ spec(Aϱ ) would satisfy |α| = λ. The determinant det(I −
zAϱ ) would vanish at z = α−1 , a point of the Perron circle, so the reciprocal
determinant and its normalized logarithm could not be holomorphic in any
neighbourhood of the closed Perron disc. This contradicts item (i). Since
Proposition 3.5 gives ρ(Aϱ ) ≤ λ for every ϱ, item (i) forces η < λ.
Item (ii) is exactly the same condition after finite character decomposition.
The zero-average class functions have no trivial character coordinate. Their
periodic logarithms are finite linear combinations of the non-trivial determinant logarithms by Theorem 3.7, and the primitive series are then obtained
from those finitely many logarithms by the Adams–Möbius formula of Corollary 3.10. Therefore the Perron values used by Theorems 3.32 and 3.38 are
available from the determinant data precisely when the non-trivial determinant branches in item (i) extend through the closed Perron disc. The
trivial channel is deliberately excluded here; it is singular at z = λ−1 and is
handled separately by the scalar finite-part normalisation.
Finally suppose the strict gap fails. Because only finitely many irreducibles occur and each non-trivial block has spectral radius at most λ,
there is a non-trivial ϱ with ρ(Aϱ ) = λ. Theorem 4.1 supplies a phase ω
and a boundary line field satisfying (117); the converse half of its proof constructs an eigenvector of the column realization with eigenvalue ωλ. The
row and column realizations have the same spectrum, so ωλ ∈ spec(Aϱ ).
Hence det(I − zAϱ ) = 0 at z = (ωλ)−1 , and the reciprocal determinant
has a boundary pole. This is precisely the obstruction detected by Proposition 4.6.
□
Corollary 4.8 (Boundary obstruction is the only eta-gap export). Under
the standing hypotheses with λ > 1, the results of this section export exactly
the following finite-state alternative to the main theorem chain:

100

HAOBO MA

(i) either no non-trivial boundary line field exists, equivalently η < λ, and
the Perron-point hypotheses of Theorems 3.32 and 3.38 are verified;
(ii) or a non-trivial boundary line field exists, equivalently η = λ, and some
non-trivial determinant factor has a pole on the Perron circle, so the
Perron-point determinant recovery required by the class-Mertens and
class-product constants is not supplied by this paper.
In particular, the loop-character and loop-subgroup statements in this section are diagnostic consequences of the boundary alternative. They are not
structural sufficient conditions for η < λ except where the stated hypotheses
explicitly eliminate every possible boundary line field.
Proof. Corollary 4.2 proves that the absence of non-trivial boundary line
fields is equivalent to η < λ, while the existence of such a line field is equivalent to η = λ. The first alternative is exactly the strict-gap hypothesis
invoked in Theorems 3.32 and 3.38. For the second alternative, Proposition 4.7 shows that the same line field produces a non-trivial eigenvalue on
the Perron circle and hence a boundary pole in the corresponding determinant factor. Therefore the non-trivial Perron branches used by the main
product theorem are available precisely in the first alternative. The loopcharacter classifier and the loop-subgroup obstruction are proved by starting
from such a boundary line field and recording necessary consequences, or by
imposing explicit hypotheses that rule out all such fields. Thus no broader
strict-gap criterion is asserted.
□
Corollary 4.9 (Finite-state eta-gap gate for the main constants). Assume G
is non-trivial and that the operational test of Proposition 4.6 has been passed,
so no non-trivial boundary line field exists. Then the following finite-state
implication is the complete eta-gap input to the main theorem chain:
(i) the strict-gap hypothesis η < λ required in Main Theorems II and III
is available;
(ii) the non-trivial determinant logarithms have exactly the Perron-point
branches certified by Proposition 4.7;
(iii) the constants MC (A, τ ; G), Fϱ (λ−1 ), and KC are then computed by the
Adams–Möbius determinant formulas, the fixed-label Euler transform,
and class-function Fourier inversion, with no additional datum from
the eta-gap section.
Conversely, if the finite-state certificate fails, the boundary-line-field alternative of Theorem 4.1 occurs and this paper supplies no replacement sufficient
condition for the missing Perron-point constants. Broader structural criteria
forcing η < λ for general cocycle classes are therefore split material, except
for the explicit line-field exclusions proved in this section.
Proof. Proposition 4.6 proves that passing the operational certificate is exactly η < λ, proving item (i), while Proposition 4.7 identifies this same
condition with the holomorphic determinant branches needed at the Perron
point, proving item (ii). The role of this condition in the article is the role
stated in Theorems 3.32 and 3.38: it permits evaluation of the non-trivial
primitive Peter–Weyl channels at z = λ−1 . Those evaluated channels are

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

101

subsequently inserted into the Fourier formulas of Proposition 3.66 and theorem 3.68 and the fixed-label Euler transform of Theorem 3.38 and propositions 3.37 and 3.56. This proves item (iii): the line-field diagnostic is used
only to decide whether this hypothesis holds, so it contributes no independent constant. If the certificate fails, Proposition 4.6 and theorem 4.1 give
a non-trivial boundary line field, and Proposition 4.7 shows that the needed
Perron branch is then obstructed. The final split-scope sentence follows
because Corollary 4.8 and proposition 4.10 state that this section proves
only the finite alternative and its explicit line-field exclusions, not a broad
sufficient-condition theory.
□
Proposition 4.10 (Finite diagnostic scope of the eta-gap input). The etagap results in this section give, for the finite edge cocycle fixed in the paper,
exactly the following conditional diagnostic and no stronger structural criterion.
(i) If the finite-state certificate of Proposition 4.6 succeeds, then η < λ,
and the Perron-point evaluations used in Main Theorems II–III are
justified by Proposition 4.7.
(ii) If the certificate fails, then some non-trivial irreducible channel admits
the boundary line field of Theorem 4.1. In that case this article makes
no class-Mertens or class-product assertion for the failed cocycle beyond the open-disc determinant identities of Main Theorem I.
(iii) The only structural sufficient conditions for the strict inequality proved
here are those obtained by excluding the classified boundary line fields,
such as the loop-character obstruction and the perfect-loop-group consequence. A broad sufficient-condition theory forcing η < λ for general
cocycle classes is outside this article.
Proof. By Proposition 4.6, success of the operational finite-state test is equivalent to the absence of a non-trivial Perron boundary channel, hence to
η < λ. Under that strict inequality, Proposition 4.7 gives exactly the zerofree Perron branches needed to evaluate the non-trivial determinant coordinates at z = λ−1 ; this proves (i).
If the same certificate fails, then Proposition 4.6 supplies a non-trivial
channel with spectral radius λ. Applying Theorem 4.1 to that channel
gives the boundary line field, and the proof of Proposition 4.7 shows that
the Perron-point continuation required by the non-trivial class-Mertens and
class-product constants is not available from the hypotheses of this paper.
Main Theorem I is unaffected because it is proved on the open Perron disc
and does not require evaluation at λ−1 . This proves (ii).
Finally, Corollaries 4.3 and 4.5 and proposition 4.4 all proceed by starting with the line field classified in Theorem 4.1 and then imposing finite
hypotheses that forbid that line field. No argument in this section proves
that an arbitrary broader cocycle class has no such line field. Therefore the
exported theorem chain is precisely the conditional one in (i)–(ii), with only
the stated finite boundary-obstruction eliminations as sufficient conditions.
This proves (iii).
□

102

HAOBO MA

Remark 4.11 (Strict-gap criteria split). The diagnostics above characterize
the boundary η = λ by a finite line-field test and provide only the hypothesis check needed for Main Theorems II–III. The loop-character classifier
Corollary 4.3 and perfect-loop-group consequence Corollary 4.5 record the
structural consequences of this boundary test that are used in the present
paper. We do not attempt here to classify all broader structural hypotheses
on the cocycle τ which force η < λ; only the finite boundary obstruction
and the perfect-loop-group elimination of that obstruction are part of the
finite-determinant product-constant theorem chain. These statements are
therefore diagnostics for a fixed finite-state input, not a promised classification of cocycles with η < λ. A general theory of structural criteria for strict
twisted spectral gaps is therefore left as a separate problem.
5. Quotient shadows and cyclic-detection boundary
This section proves structural consequences of the finite-group theorems
after the constants in Main Theorems II–III have already been constructed.
Quotient functoriality gives the abelianised primitive profile; the Peter–
Weyl splitting then separates the one-dimensional shadow from the higherdimensional defect. The final S3 model is a certificate for the Adams obstruction, distinguishing the quadratic sign-channel effect from the certified
standard-channel non-abelian defect. Cyclic quotients of the fixed cocycle
are kept separate from the canonical cyclic-lift root-of-unity layer used later
in Section 6; neither construction is treated here as data varying over graph
resolutions or folds. None of these quotient-shadow results supplies a new
product constant; they recover and test the constants already fixed by the
Main theorem chain. Throughout this section, “boundary” means the exact linear-algebraic boundary of what cyclic quotients can detect inside the
class-function space of G.
5.1. Quotient functoriality and the abelian shadow.

orthonormal basis of the class-function space and 1G is the trivial basis
vector, this expansion is orthogonal to 1G . Hence ⟨BA,τ ;G , 1G ⟩G = 0, and
the two asserted projection statements follow.
For two cocycles over the same A, the scalar term M(A)1G is identical
in the two decompositions. Subtracting the two copies of (121) and using
(120) for each cocycle gives (122). Orthogonality of the irreducible character
basis then gives the stated if and only if: equality of the normalised class
constants is equality of the class-function expansions, hence equality of every
non-trivial Fourier coefficient, and the converse is immediate from the same
expansion.
Finally, the quantities FP(A), M(A), FA , {Ψq (A)}q≥1 , and the root-ofunity layers in Section 6 are functions of the single scalar matrix A. In the
Main Theorem II decomposition just proved, the only term depending solely
on A is M(A)1G . The remaining term is the non-trivial Peter–Weyl expansion (120), whose coefficients are the cocycle-dependent boundary values
Πτϱ (λ−1 ).
It remains to prove the family classification. Since A and G are fixed,
every listed scalar record has the same value for every τ ∈ T. Thus the map
τ 7→ M#
A,τ ;G is constant on T if and only if the right-hand side of
M#
A,τ ;G = M(A)1G + BA,τ ;G
is independent of τ , which is equivalent to BA,τ ;G being independent of τ .
This proves the equivalence of (i) and (ii).
The equivalence of (ii) and (iii) is exactly the uniqueness of Fourier coefficients in the irreducible-character basis applied to (120): equality of the
class functions BA,τ ;G is equivalent to equality of every coefficient Πτϱ (λ−1 )
with ϱ ̸= 1. This proves (123). The final obstruction statement follows by
taking a two-element family T = {τ, τ ′ }. The scalar records agree because
they depend only on A, while the full profiles disagree by the just-proved
equivalence. This proves the claimed boundary for scalar fixed-matrix inputs.
□
Corollary 5.3 (No scalar cyclic-lift descent without a constant class profile). Fix A, G, and a non-empty family T ⊂ Tgap (A, G). Let R(A) be any
collection of fixed-matrix scalar records formed from A, for instance FP(A),
M(A), FA , the branch-lifted cyclic-lift record {Ψq (A)}q≥1 , the torsion layers {FA (ωqj )}0≤j<q , or the fixed-matrix exponential record of Definition 2.8.
These records are attached to the single matrix A, with no fold map, resolution change, or cocycle descent structure included. Then the following are
equivalent.
(i) The fixed data R(A), regarded as a candidate record for the family T,
determine a single normalised primitive class profile on T.
(ii) The class-profile map τ 7→ BA,τ ;G is constant on T.

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

105

(iii) For every non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G), the Perron-point primitive channel
Πτϱ (λ−1 ) is independent of τ ∈ T.
Consequently, if two gap cocycles over the same matrix A have distinct nontrivial Perron-point primitive channels, then no fixed-matrix cyclic-lift record
of A descends to a stable cross-cocycle class-profile invariant on the two-point
family they form. Any stronger invariant requires additional descent data
beyond the fixed-matrix records of Definition 2.8.
Proof. Since R(A) is formed only from the scalar matrix A, it has the same
value for every τ ∈ T. Thus it can determine at most one candidate class
profile on T. It determines the actual normalised primitive class profile on
the whole family exactly when BA,τ ;G is independent of τ . This proves the
equivalence of (i) and (ii). The equivalence of (ii) and (iii) is the family
classification in Theorem 5.2. The final assertion follows by applying these
equivalences to T = {τ, τ ′ } and using the hypothesis that at least one nontrivial Perron-point primitive channel differs.
□

We now fix the separation between two cyclic operations. First, one may

5.3. The exact boundary of cyclic detection. The previous theorem
identifies the canonical splitting. The next statement concerns the span obtained by using all cyclic quotient observables together. It says that this
aggregate cyclic information is exactly the one-dimensional sector. An individual cyclic quotient sees only the one-dimensional characters that factor
through that quotient; here we take the span over all cyclic quotients. This
concerns quotient pullbacks on the fixed cocycle, not the canonical cyclic
lift of Section 6; no root-of-unity arithmetic for non-abelian sectors is being
asserted here.
For later use, write Ccyc (G) for the linear span of the pullbacks q ∗ f , where
q : G ↠ Z/mZ ranges over all cyclic quotients and f ranges over the class
functions on Z/mZ.
Lemma 5.9 (Cyclic quotients and one-dimensional characters). One has
Ccyc (G) = span{χ ∈ Irr(G) : dim χ = 1}.
Proof. Let q : G ↠ Z/mZ be a cyclic quotient and let f be a class function
on Z/mZ. Since Z/mZ is abelian, its irreducible characters form a basis of
its class functions, so
X

fb(ψ)ψ.

q∗f =

ψ∈Z/mZ

and each ψ ◦ q is a one-dimensional character of G. Hence
Ccyc (G) ⊂ span{χ ∈ Irr(G) : dim χ = 1}.
Conversely, let χ ∈ Irr(G) be one-dimensional. Its image χ(G) ⊂ C× is a
finite subgroup of the multiplicative group of complex numbers, hence cyclic.

Let qχ : G ↠ χ(G) be the induced quotient map, and let ιχ : χ(G) ,→ C×
be the tautological character. Then

so χ ∈ Ccyc (G). This proves the reverse inclusion.

Remark 5.12 (Minimal conjugation-error detector). Proposition 5.11 is precisely the counterexample described in the proof of Proposition 3.2: for
G = C3 , the correct class-indicator formula requires the conjugate (or equivalently the group inverse), and the failure without it sends every class {a} to
the wrong class {a−1 }. In the subsequent S3 example all characters happen
to be real-valued, so this failure does not manifest there; the C3 check is
therefore the minimal test that can detect a conjugation error.
5.5. The S3 obstruction certificate. The preceding structural theorems
leave one finite-state question open: whether the higher-dimensional defect
can actually occur in a small symbolic skew product and whether it survives at the product-constant level of Main Theorem III. The answer is
yes: the decisive certificate is the pair of fixed-label Euler-transform values Fε (1/2) and Fρ (1/2), with the primitive-channel intervals supplying the
cyclic-detection recovery. We record the witness here and place the routine matrix arithmetic, primitive coefficient extraction, and rational interval
checks in Appendices C and D. For readability, the certificate chain is as
follows. The finite model, twisted gap, and vanishing sign Artin logarithm
are checked in Proposition C.2; the primitive fixed-label formulae are computed in Proposition C.3; the Perron-point primitive windows are certified
in Lemma C.5, using the rational logarithm estimates of Lemma D.1; and
the product-level fixed-label windows used in Main Theorem III are certified
in Lemma D.2.
Lemma 5.13 (S3 certificate summary used in the main proof). In the certified S3 full-shift model below, the only numerical inputs imported from the
appendices are the following rational windows:
(i) −342/1000 < Πε (1/2) < −341/1000 and −719/1000 < Πρ (1/2) <
−718/1000, as stated in (154) and proved from the exact rational
truncation-and-tail bounds (166)– (169) in Lemma D.1;
(ii) −381/1000 < Fε (1/2) < −380/1000 and −923/1000 < Fρ (1/2) <
−922/1000, as stated in (174) and (175) and proved in Lemma D.2
by rational logarithm enclosures and rational tail bounds.
These four intervals are exact enough for the main-text obstruction because
the first pair excludes zero in the primitive sign and standard channels,
while the second pair excludes both zero and the sign-channel Artin value
Lε (1/2) = 0. No decimal approximation is used in the proof of the obstruction theorem; every endpoint above is a rational number and every strict
inequality is inherited from the cited appendix lemma.
Proof. The appendix lemma Lemma D.1 proves rational lower and upper
bounds for the finite logarithmic sums and for the remaining positive tails in
the primitive sign and standard channels. The proof of Lemma C.5 imports
precisely those bounds and records the coarser rational windows in (154);
these are item (i).
The appendix lemma Lemma D.2 applies the same rational logarithm
enclosures to the fixed-label Euler-transform sums and records the rational
windows (174) and (175); these are item (ii). Since the displayed intervals

112

HAOBO MA

are open rational intervals lying strictly on the negative side of zero, the
primitive windows prove non-vanishing of Πε (1/2) and Πρ (1/2), and the
fixed-label windows prove non-vanishing of Fε (1/2) and Fρ (1/2). Finally
Proposition C.2 proves Lε (z) ≡ 0, so the fixed-label sign window also proves
Fε (1/2) ̸= Lε (1/2). These are exactly the numerical hypotheses used below.
□

The two characters are real-valued, so complex conjugation has no effect;

Since both intervals avoid zero, both non-trivial coordinates are present.

additional obstruction could live. Since Lε (1/2) = 0 by Theorem 5.14, the
non-zero sign coordinate implies Fε (1/2) ̸= Lε (1/2), and the obstruction
criterion Theorem 3.63 gives a non-zero Artin-substitution error.
Finally, a class function factors through the abelianisation S3 ↠ C2 if and
only if it lies in the span of the two one-dimensional characters 1 and ε. Character orthogonality puts ρ in the orthogonal complement of that span. The
certified inequality Fρ (1/2) ̸= 0 therefore gives a genuine standard-sector
component invisible to all abelian, equivalently cyclic, quotient class observables. Hence the cyclic quotient data do not determine the full productconstant vector.
□
Corollary 5.16 (Product-constant obstruction is already finite-state). In
the certified S3 full-shift model of Theorem 5.14, the obstruction to replacing
the Adams-corrected constants of Main Theorem III by periodic Artin logarithms is completely detected by the two finite rational windows for Fε (1/2)
and Fρ (1/2). In particular, no cyclic quotient recovery can recover the product vector (KC )C⊂S3 : the sign window already forces a non-zero normalized
Artin-substitution error for some class, and the standard window lies in the
non-abelian Peter–Weyl sector orthogonal to every cyclic quotient observable.
Proof. The first assertion is the fixed-label part of Theorem 5.14 together
with the determinant rigidity and obstruction criterion Theorems 3.41
and 3.63: the normalized class-product vector is the Peter–Weyl expansion
whose non-trivial coordinates are the fixed-label values Fϱ (1/2), and
equality with the naive Artin vector would force Fϱ (1/2) = Lϱ (1/2) for
every non-trivial ϱ. The certified sign interval and Lε (1/2) = 0 therefore
give a non-zero discrepancy; Corollary 3.64 converts it into the stated
classwise normalized lower bound. The second assertion follows from Theorems 5.8 and 5.10: cyclic quotient observables see only the one-dimensional
characters of S3 , while the certified non-zero Fρ (1/2) is in the standard
two-dimensional Peter–Weyl sector.
□
6. Scalar normalisation
Throughout this section, A denotes a primitive non-negative real matrix
with Perron root λ > 1. The symbols pn (A) denote the algebraic Möbius
coefficients of (1); they are primitive orbit counts only in the integer multigraph case fixed in Section 2. For the finite-group results, the scalar role was
delimited in Section 1.2: only the trivial-channel finite part M(A) is used.
We therefore keep the finite-part formula in full and record the lift comparison only in a compressed fixed-matrix form; its determinant manipulations
and supporting proofs are deferred to Appendix A.

6.2. Scalar interface for the finite-group results. The remaining scalar
material is used only as an interface for the finite-group determinant and
product results. It is not a source of non-trivial class-product coordinates:
by Theorem 3.41, those coordinates are exactly the fixed-label Peter–Weyl
Perron values. The canonical cyclic lift A⟨q⟩ = A ⊗ Pq from Equation (11),
the branch-lifted logarithmic record {Ψq (A)}q≥1 , and the root-of-unity layers
of FA are fixed-matrix records. Their reconstruction theorem is proved in
Appendix A; the main text uses them only through the following boundary
statement.

Corollary 6.5 (Only the trivial channel descends from fixed-matrix scalar
data). In the setting of Theorem 6.4, the projection of the normalised classMertens profile onto the constant class functions is exactly M(A)1G . Hence
any scalar finite-part formula or canonical cyclic-lift reconstruction that depends only on A can enter the finite-group theorem chain only through the
trivial-channel normalisation M(A). It cannot determine the non-trivial
class profile for a family T unless the non-trivial Perron-point channels
Πτϱ (λ−1 ), ϱ ̸= 1, are already independent of τ on that family.
Proof. The splitting (134) writes the normalised profile as the sum of the
constant class function M(A)1G and BA,τ ;G . By definition of BA,τ ;G in Definition 5.1, the latter has only non-trivial Peter–Weyl coefficients, namely the
values Πτϱ (λ−1 ) with ϱ ̸= 1. Orthogonality of irreducible characters therefore identifies the constant projection with M(A)1G and the non-constant
projection with the displayed non-trivial channels. A record depending only
on A is constant as τ varies, so it can determine this non-constant projection throughout T exactly in the independence case already characterized in
Theorem 6.4.
□
Corollary 6.6 (Rank-zero scalar obstruction for class profiles). Keep the
notation and hypotheses of Theorem 6.4. Let T ⊂ Tgap (A, G) be non-empty,
and let
Z(T) := spanR {BA,τ ;G − BA,τ ′ ;G : τ, τ ′ ∈ T}
be the real zero-mean class-function space actually swept out by the nontrivial primitive profiles in the family. Then the scalar finite part FP(A),
the scalar Mertens normalisation M(A), the reduced determinant C(A), and
every canonical cyclic-lift fixed-matrix record named in Appendix A have rank
zero on Z(T): they are constant on T and annihilate all profile differences.
Consequently, any finite collection of real linear invariants whose restriction to profile differences is linear and which, together with the scalar records,
recovers the normalised class-Mertens profile of Main Theorem II on T must
have real rank at least dim Z(T) on the non-trivial Peter–Weyl sector. The
same lower bound applies to any such linear recovery procedure for the product constants (KC )C⊂G of Main Theorem III after the scalar product normalisation is fixed. In particular, when the profiles in T affinely span the
whole zero-mean class-function space, at least #{conjugacy classes of G} − 1
independent non-scalar coordinates are necessary, and scalar finite-matrix
records supply none of them.

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

119

Proof. The records listed in the statement depend only on the matrix A,
not on the cocycle τ . They are therefore constant on the family T. By
Equation (134), for every τ, τ ′ ∈ T,
#
M#
A,τ ;G − MA,τ ′ ;G = BA,τ ;G − BA,τ ′ ;G .

The right-hand side is a zero-mean class function, since BA,τ ;G has only
non-trivial Peter–Weyl coefficients by Definition 5.1 and (120). Thus scalar
records see no vector in Z(T), which is the asserted rank-zero statement.
Let I be any additional finite real invariants whose restriction to profile
differences is linear and which, after adjoining the scalar records, recovers
the normalised class-Mertens profile on T. Since the scalar part is constant
on T, the induced linear difference map must distinguish every vector in
Z(T). Therefore its restriction to the real vector space Z(T) is injective,
and elementary linear algebra forces its rank to be at least dim Z(T).
For product constants, Theorem 3.38 and Corollary 3.42 identify the logarithmic product vector, after scalar normalisation, with the same zero-mean
Peter–Weyl boundary coordinates. Thus recovering (KC )C on T is equivalent to recovering the corresponding zero-mean profile differences, so the
same rank lower bound applies. If the affine span is the whole zero-mean
class-function space, its dimension is the number of conjugacy classes of G
minus one, because the full real class-function space has one coordinate per
conjugacy class and the scalar direction is one-dimensional.
□
The fixed-matrix reconstruction and one-layer Fourier inversion statements
are Theorems A.7 and A.8 in Appendix A. They identify only the scalar
comparison model and, by the fixed-matrix qualification following Theorem A.7, do not descend to the non-trivial branch-compatible class-product
coordinates. They are not used in the Adams–Möbius finite-group arguments; Corollary 3.42 records the corresponding finite-state obstruction on
the product side.
7. Discussion and open problems
7.1. Summary of the main contribution. For a finite-group extension
of a mixing subshift of finite type, the twisted determinant family records
periodic labels. The main point of this paper is that the corresponding
primitive labels are recovered by a finite Adams–Möbius calculation in Peter–
Weyl coordinates. This is the finite-state correction forced by the repetition
rule g 7→ g m for primitive orbits. Under the twisted gap η < λ, the recovered
primitive channels have Perron-point values and hence determine the classindexed constants MC (A, τ ; G) in the Frobenius-class Mertens sums.
The Euler-product constants require the same distinction. Once the classical leading exponent |C|/|G| has been separated, the constant is governed
by the fixed-label Euler transform Fϱ (λ−1 ), not by the periodic Artin logarithm Lϱ (λ−1 ). The determinant formula therefore gives


KC = exp−





|C| 

χ∗ϱ (C)Fϱ (λ−1 ) ,
|G|
ϱ̸=1
X

120

HAOBO MA

with the positive real branch (Corollaries 3.10 and 3.59, theorems 3.32, 3.38
and 3.68, and proposition 3.56). This is a determinant-level refinement of
the classical Chebotarev/Mertens orbit-counting picture, rather than a new
leading-density theorem. Quotient functoriality identifies the abelianised
primitive profile, and cyclic quotient observables span precisely the onedimensional Peter–Weyl shadow. The S3 example shows both phenomena
concretely: the sign line detects an Adams correction invisible to the Artin
logarithm, while the standard two-dimensional representation gives a genuine non-abelian component.
The scalar finite-part and cyclic-lift calculations supply the trivial-channel
normalisation and a fixed-matrix comparison model. The projection theorem Theorem 5.2 marks the boundary: after normalising the class constants,
scalar data give the constant class-function component M(A)1G , whereas
the non-trivial primitive profile is the Peter–Weyl vector BA,τ ;G .
7.2. Scope boundaries. The results above are intentionally finitedimensional. The strict twisted gap η < λ is used as a finite-state
hypothesis, with Proposition 4.6 serving as a verification tool; the paper
diagnoses the boundary case η = λ but does not prove broad structural
criteria forcing a strict gap for arbitrary cocycles. Likewise, no Hölder
transfer-operator determinant, Ruelle-resonance theorem, pressure-window
statement, completed zeta normalisation, xi-structure, functional equation,
or external determinant-normalisation theorem is imported into the proof
of Main Theorems I–III.
The scalar cyclic-lift appendices should be read only as appendix-level
finite-state comparison material around the constants already established
in Main Theorems I–III, not as a non-abelian root-of-unity sampling theory, a positive cross-resolution descent theorem, or a standalone reducedspectrum inverse problem. Secondary spectral-edge tail refinements are retained only as conditional appendix material after those constants have been
fixed; their interface with the main line is the non-interference statement
Proposition 3.61, not a second proof of Main Theorems I–III. The explicit
S3 certificate is a witness for the Adams correction in this determinant model
and is used as a finite tool only; no general finite-group certificate taxonomy,
certificate-complexity theory, or universality hierarchy is claimed in this article.
Natural extensions to Hölder/Ruelle transfer determinants, completed
zeta and xi normalisations, structural strict-gap criteria, secondary-edge theory, fixed-matrix cyclic-lift inverse problems, sibling Fredholm/kernel and
language-obstruction packages, or broader computational certification are
therefore left to separate projects rather than folded into the finite-state
theorem chain proved here.
Appendix A. Appendix: scalar cyclic-lift comparison
This appendix collects the scalar cyclic-lift comparison arguments behind
the normalisation and comparison statements in the main text. It is retained
as appendix-only support; its reconstruction statements are fixed-matrix
scalar facts, not additional finite-group class-product theorems. Its interface
with the theorem chain is narrow: it supports the trivial-channel finite part

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

121

M(A), the canonical cyclic-lift multiple-sum identity, and the fixed-matrix
branch comparison used to separate scalar data from non-trivial Peter–Weyl
class profiles. Main Theorems I–III use the finite-group determinant and
Adams–Möbius machinery in the main text for the non-trivial class information; scalar cyclic-lift data alone do not provide a descent theorem for
non-abelian primitive class data, cross-resolution invariants, or fold-stable
scalar records. A broader cyclic-lift inverse-spectral theory would require
a separate paper. In particular, a shortened submission may split this appendix off after citing only the scalar normalisation, the canonical cyclic-lift
multiple-sum identity, and the fixed-matrix branch comparison; no proof of
Main Theorems I–III below uses the appendix as an independent source of
non-trivial class-product coordinates.
The remaining appendices are support material for the finite-state Adams–
Möbius argument. Main Theorems I–III form the closed theorem chain; the
appendices supply scalar normalisations, boundary checks, and the explicit
S3 rational witness used at the indicated points.

for cocycles, extensions, or non-trivial Peter–Weyl class profiles.

Appendix support does not descend to class-product coordinates. The reconstruction in Theorem A.7 is a fixed-matrix scalar reconstruction. More precisely, fix the same primitive matrix A and compare any two finite-group
extensions over that base, possibly with different cocycles and different finite
groups. Their scalar cyclic-lift records (Ψq (A))q≥1 in the theorem are identical whenever the same branch-lifted logarithms are supplied, because the
record is attached only to A. Consequently no function of this fixed-matrix
cyclic-lift record alone can distinguish two such extensions whose non-trivial
branch-compatible fixed-label Perron coordinate vectors (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 differ.
In particular, the theorem is not a finite-group class-product reconstruction
theorem and supplies no replacement for the determinant coordinates of
Proposition 3.56.
The data in Theorem A.7 are exactly the branch-lifted logarithms
Ψq (A) ∈ log(qCλ (A⟨q⟩ )) for the canonical cyclic lifts of the one matrix
A. They contain no cocycle, no finite group, no character table, no
Adams tensor, and no twisted determinant det(I − zAπ ) for a non-trivial
representation. Hence two extensions over the same base matrix, when
supplied with the same logarithmic branches for this scalar record, give the

128

HAOBO MA

same input to the appendix theorem. If their non-trivial fixed-label Perron
vectors are different, any function of the common scalar input takes the
same value on both extensions and therefore cannot recover or distinguish
those vectors. The determinant-coordinate recovery in the main theorem
chain uses the additional finite-group data listed in Proposition 3.56, so it
is not a consequence of this appendix theorem alone.
□

Appendix B. Scalar boundary checks for the normalisation
B.1. Parameter-varying records. The reconstruction statements of Section 6 are fixed-matrix statements. They recover data either from the full
record

{FA (ωqj )}0≤j≤q−1 ,

The following scalar facts are checks on the boundary of the scalar normalisation used by the finite-group theorem chain. They are not extra classproduct hypotheses. Holomorphic variation and effective determinant truncation provide auxiliary control of the trivial-channel finite part, while the
Adams–Möbius and class-product theorems use from this appendix only the
fixed-matrix normalisation M(A) = γ + FP(A) and the canonical cyclic-lift

λ(θ) contributes the factor 1−λ(θ)1−m , which is non-zero because |λ(θ)| > 1,
and every other eigenvalue ν contributes 1 − νλ(θ)−m , with
|νλ(θ)−m | ≤ κ|λ(θ)|1−m ≤ κλ1−m
< 1.

Thus the determinants in the displayed series are holomorphic and nonvanishing on V . Again V is simply connected, so their logarithms have
branches, fixed by the chosen branch at θ0 .

rigidity part of Main Theorem III.

Corollary B.5 (Finite input–output certificate for the scalar finite part).
Under the hypotheses of Theorem B.4, fix a tolerance ϵ > 0. The finite
certificate inputs are the matrix A, its Perron root λ > 1, the scalar normalising constant log C(A), the determinant polynomial det(I − zA), and a
trace majorant Ktr (A). Choose a truncation level M ≥ 2 for which
Ktr (A)λ−M
≤ ϵ/2.
(M + 1)(1 − λ−M )(1 − λ−1 )
If IM ⊂ R is any rational interval enclosing the finite determinant sum
FPM (A), with numerical radius Rnum ≤ ϵ/2 − BM (A), then

(136)

(137)

BM (A) :=

FP(A) ∈ IM + [−BM (A) − Rnum , BM (A) + Rnum ].

After adding γ, the same interval encloses M(A). This scalar certificate
depends on λ only through the tail bound and on the finite determinant polynomial through the finitely many logarithms 2 ≤ m ≤ M ; unlike the finitegroup certificate in Proposition 3.58, it has no dependence on a twisted gap
η or on a finite group G.
Proof. The search for M terminates because λ > 1, so the bound BM (A)
tends to zero. Theorem B.4 gives |FP(A) − FPM (A)| ≤ BM (A). Since IM
encloses FPM (A) with numerical radius Rnum , adding the two independent
radii gives (137). The statement for M(A) follows by translating the interval
by γ. All logarithms in FPM (A) are ordinary interior-disc determinant logarithms, because m ≥ 2 gives ρ(λ−m A) < 1. Hence no finite-group character
table, class-power map, non-trivial Abel branch, or twisted spectral-gap parameter enters this scalar certificate.
□
B.2. Arithmetic boundary. The cyclic-lift formulas in Section 6 recover
FA and the relative non-Perron spectrum from scalar root-of-unity data. In
this article those formulas are retained only as appendix-level scalar normalisation and comparison tools. Arithmetic refinements of the same data
remain statements about the canonical cyclic lift of the base matrix; cyclic

134

HAOBO MA

quotients of a fixed cocycle, treated in Section 5, are different functorial
pullbacks and detect only the one-dimensional Peter–Weyl sector.
B.3. Growing-family boundary. The finite-part identities also yield estimates for growing families of primitive matrices under additional hypotheses on Λ(A)/λ, d, and λ. In the present fixed-matrix class-product theorem
these estimates document the scalar normalisation regime; the finite-group
Adams–Frobenius mechanism uses only the fixed matrix and the stated
twisted spectral gap.
Appendix C. Detailed computations for the S3 obstruction
This appendix contains the matrix calculations and coefficient extractions
used in Section 5. The main text keeps only the structural obstruction theorem; the computational certificates are recorded here to keep the quotientshadow calculations from obscuring the Adams–Möbius class-Mertens argument. The appendix verifies one finite obstruction witness, not a catalogue or classification of small non-abelian examples. The opening quadratic
Adams obstruction is intentionally a proposition, not a theorem-level main
result; all references use the proposition label accordingly.

Remark D.3 (Exact-arithmetic reproducibility). The mathematical certificate in this appendix is the displayed rational arithmetic in Lemmas D.1
and D.2. A reader or auxiliary exact-arithmetic program can reproduce the
calculation by computing the rational logarithm endpoints L3 (q) ≤ log q ≤
U3 (q), multiplying them by the signed rational coefficients in E7 , R15 , Q8 ,
and S32 , adding the four rational tails, and clearing positive denominators
in the four final interval comparisons. The local reproducibility files are part
of the source record:

Appendix E. Appendix: local finite reproducibility records
for the S3 witness
This appendix records the local finite reproducibility formats used by
Proposition 3.58. The main text uses only the finite determinant computability theorem and the determinant-to-product Fourier map; the tabular
records below are included to make the concrete S3 determinant constants
auditable. They are not promoted to a fourth main theorem chain or to a
general certified-computation framework for arbitrary finite groups.
Proposition E.1 (Finite rational reproducibility record). Assume the hypotheses of Theorem 3.32. Fix a non-trivial ϱ ∈ Irr(G), a tolerance ϵ > 0,
and a truncation level M satisfying (89). The output of Proposition 3.58
is reproduced by the following finite data. The record consists only of finite
algebraic model data and rational inequalities of the following four kinds:
(i) the list of triples (r, m, π) with rm ≤ M , with the scalar Perron triple
(1, 1, 1) deleted;
(m)
(ii) the Adams coefficients aϱ,π = ⟨ψ m χϱ , χπ ⟩G , written from the finite
character table and class-power table;
(iii) for every listed triple, a branch tag specifying either the Abel non-trivial
Perron branch (rm = 1, π ̸= 1) or the ordinary germ value inside the
Perron disc (rm ≥ 2), together with a rational rectangular interval
enclosure for Log det(I − λ−rm Aπ )−1 ;
(iv) two rational error bounds RM and Rnum : the tail bound RM is certified
either directly from (91) or from the rational envelope (92), and Rnum
is certified from the finite interval-propagation bound (93).
Any checker which verifies these four records by exact algebraic comparison and rational interval arithmetic produces a rational rectangular interval
(M )
IM ⊂ C enclosing the finite sum Fϱ
and certifies the inclusion
(176)

Fϱ (λ−1 ) ∈ IM + {w ∈ C : |w| ≤ RM + Rnum } ,

where all sides of IM and both radii are rational. Consequently the mathematical reproducibility support is the displayed finite record itself; any external file or script for a concrete model is only an optional serialization
of the same rational checks used in Proposition 3.58 and corollary 3.59. No
claim is made here that this record is a polished software package or a universal certified-computation infrastructure for arbitrary finite groups, arbitrary
matrix families, or arbitrary branch conventions.

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

147

Proof. The first two records are finite because the set {(r, m) : rm ≤ M }
and Irr(G) are finite, and because the Adams coefficients are finite charactertable inner products. The deleted scalar triple is exactly the omitted term
in Proposition 3.58; its coefficient is zero for ϱ ̸= 1, so no certificate record
must assign a finite value to the singular scalar logarithm.
For each remaining triple, the branch tag is one of the two cases already
proved in item (d) of Proposition 3.58: either the non-trivial Abel branch at
rm = 1, made regular by the strict twisted gap, or the ordinary logarithmic
germ at rm ≥ 2, where ρ(λ−rm Aπ ) < 1. Thus every tagged interval encloses
a uniquely specified logarithm. Multiplying the rational rectangular inter(m)
vals by the certified coefficients µ(m)aϱ,π /(rm), enclosing each algebraic
product by exact comparison in a rational rectangle, and summing those
rectangles gives a rational rectangular interval IM which contains the stated
finite truncation. Its excess radius is at most Rnum by the triangle inequality
used in (93). The discarded terms with rm > M have total absolute value at
most the rational number RM , either because the certificate compares RM
with the algebraic tail in (91), or because it records rational θ, Qϱ satisfying
(92) and RM ≥ Qϱ θM +1 /(1 − θ). All these checks are finite algebraic or rational inequalities. Adding the two independent radii proves (176). Finally,
Corollary 3.59 applies finite Fourier inversion to the certified fixed-label Perron vector, so the same certificate record serializes the product constants
rather than introducing a new analytic premise.
□
Proposition E.2 (Finite input–output certificate summary). Assume the
hypotheses of Theorem 3.32, and fix a non-trivial irreducible character ϱ.
The following finite record is sufficient, and is necessary for the certificates
used in this paper, to certify the fixed-label Perron coordinate Fϱ (λ−1 ) to any
prescribed rational accuracy:
(i) the finite determinant data A, λ, G, the character table, the power
maps on conjugacy classes, and the determinant polynomials Dπ (z) =
det(I − zAπ );
(m)
(ii) a finite window M and the finitely many Adams coefficients aϱ,π with
rm ≤ M , with the sole scalar Perron triple (r, m, π) = (1, 1, 1) deleted
before any logarithm is evaluated;
(iii) for every remaining triple, the branch tag fixed by the germ at z = 0:
Abel continuation to z = λ−1 when rm = 1 and π ̸= 1, and the
ordinary interior-disc value at z = λ−rm when rm ≥ 2;
(iv) rational rectangular enclosures for these finitely many logarithms and
a rational number R dominating
both interval-propagation error and
P
the determinant tail Cϱ k>M d(k)λ1−k /k.
From this record one obtains a rational rectangle J ⊂ C with Fϱ (λ−1 ) ∈ J
and diameter bounded by the requested tolerance. Conversely, every certificate accepted by Proposition 3.58 reduces to such a record after replacing its
two radii RM and Rnum by RM + Rnum . Through finite Fourier inversion in
Theorem 3.41 and corollary 3.59, the same finite certificate determines the
corresponding certified enclosure for every Adams-corrected Frobenius-class
product constant.

148

HAOBO MA

Proof. The sufficiency direction is exactly the finite truncation theorem in
compressed form. Items (i) and (ii) are finite by the finiteness of Irr(G),
the finite character table, and the finite set {(r, m) : rm ≤ M }. The scalar
Perron triple is deleted by Propositions 3.56 and 3.58 because its coefficient is zero for ϱ ̸= 1. Item (iii) is the two-branch convention proved in
Proposition 3.58: the non-trivial boundary terms are regular Abel limits
by the twisted spectral gap, while all terms with rm ≥ 2 lie strictly inside
the Perron disc. Summing the certified finite rectangles with coefficients
(m)
(M )
µ(m)aϱ,π /(rm) gives an enclosure of Fϱ , and enlarging by the rational
radius in item (iv) contains the discarded tail by (91). Choosing M from
(89) and refining the finite interval arithmetic gives any prescribed rational
diameter.
Conversely, the certificate consists precisely of the determinant record,
the finite window and Adams coefficients, the branch-tagged finite logarithm
enclosures, and the two rational radii appearing in (176); replacing those two
radii by their sum produces item (iv) without changing any mathematical
content. The last assertion follows because the passage from the certified
non-trivial Perron vector to the class-product constants is a finite charactertable computation in Theorem 3.41 and corollary 3.59.
□

the five fields above after harmless reordering of the finite window. Thus the

Proof. Starting from the four records in Proposition 3.58, put the base matrix, Perron algebraic number, group table, class table, character table, and
class-power table in the model field. The window field is the finite triple
list in record (i) together with M and ϵ. The Adams field is record (ii).
The branch field is record (iii). The bound field is record (iv). Therefore
the schema contains no datum beyond the finite determinant reproducibility
proposition.
The verifier checks listed in the last column are precisely the proof steps
of Proposition 3.58: finite character orthogonality recomputes the Adams
coefficients; the deleted scalar triple is the unique singular Perron logarithm
and has zero coefficient for ϱ ̸= 1; the two branch tags are the two regularity
(m)
cases in Proposition 3.58; interval multiplication by µ(m)aϱ,π /(rm) and
finite summation give radius Rnum ; and the discarded triples with rm > M
contribute at most RM . Hence acceptance gives (176).
Conversely, any four-record certificate of the kind used here already specifies the same model data, finite window, Adams coefficients, branch intervals, and two radii; ordering the finite triple list lexicographically is the only
normalization needed to place it in the displayed fields. The cited S3 files
instantiate these fields with exact rational arithmetic, so their role is a serialized transcript of this finite verification rather than a template for an
arbitrary finite-group certification framework.

Appendix F. Appendix: conditional secondary spectral-edge
tail refinement
This optional appendix records a conditional tail refinement after the
class constants have been constructed. It is not part of the proof spine of
the paper. Main Theorems I–III determine Πϱ (λ−1 ), MC (A, τ ; G), Fϱ (λ−1 ),
and KC before the hypotheses of this section are introduced. If, in addition
to λ > 1 and η < λ, one has R > λ1/2 and, for the sharper tail estimate,
Γsec < R, the finite Peter–Weyl trace formula identifies a first oscillating
remainder around those constants. Thus the section is kept as appendixonly support material: it refines discarded finite-length tails under extra
spectral-edge separation, and asserts no secondary expansion without those
additional hypotheses.
Proposition F.1 (Non-interference of conditional secondary tails). The
results in this appendix are logically downstream from Main Theorems I–III.
More precisely, no statement in this section is used to define or prove any
of
Πϱ (λ−1 ),
MC (A, τ ; G),

without changing Main Theorems I–III or any constant appearing in them.
Equivalently, the body of the paper is a closed theorem chain before this appendix is invoked, and the secondary data lie in the kernel of the limiting
map to the main product constants. Thus this appendix is an instance of the
non-interference proposition Proposition 3.61, not an additional route to the
Adams–Möbius/Frobenius-product determinant chain.
Proof. The definitions used below start with the already constructed finite
matrices Aϱ , the already imposed gap η < λ, and the additional edge quantities R, E1 , Eϱ , and Γsec . None of these edge quantities occurs in the hypotheses or conclusions of Main Theorems I–III or in the formulas defining
Πϱ (λ−1 ), MC (A, τ ; G), Fϱ (λ−1 ), or KC . Theorem F.3 gives only a pointwise secondary remainder and a compensated harmonic-tail identity after
those constants have been named. Proposition F.4 then inserts that identity into the product expansion and shows that the additional factor tends
to one. Thus the hypotheses of Proposition 3.61 are met with SC (n) = sC (n)
and TC (N ) = TC (N ). The isolation theorem Theorem F.5 records the same
non-interference with the main constants. Hence the section contributes only
the conditional tail refinement after the finite determinant recovery, Perronpoint constants, and Adams-corrected product constants of the main chain
have already been established.
□

Theorem F.5 (Isolation of the secondary edge). Assume the full secondary
hypotheses λ > 1, η < λ, R > λ1/2 , and Γsec < R, equivalently the hypotheses
of Theorem F.3. The conditional secondary refinement is separated from
Main Theorems I–III in the following precise sense; it is a support-only
remainder statement, not an alternative spectral theorem for the product
constants.
(i) Its only inputs beyond Main Theorems I–III are the finite edge sets E1 ,
Eϱ and the strict separations R > λ1/2 , Γsec < R.
(ii) It does not change any value Πϱ (λ−1 ), MC (A, τ ; G), Fϱ (λ−1 ), or KC
constructed earlier.
(iii) After those main constants are fixed, all class-level secondary information is equivalent, for each n ≥ 1, to the finite Peter–Weyl edge
skeleton (E1 (n), (Eϱ (n))ϱ̸=1 ) of Proposition F.2; hence it introduces
no additional determinant, branch, cyclic-lift, or product-constant coordinate.

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

155

Proof. The first assertion is exactly the hypothesis list at the beginning of
this section together with the additional strict inequality in Theorem F.3.
The main theorem sequence supplies the determinant formula, Abel-path
Perron values, Mertens constants, and product constants before any spectraledge set is introduced here.
For (ii), Proposition F.4 proves that the secondary contribution enters the
finite product only through the factor displayed in (183). The tail TC (N )
tends to zero, and the error term in that formula tends to zero. Equivalently,
Proposition 3.61 applies to the present finite-label data, so the limiting constants remain the same MC (A, τ ; G) and KC obtained from Theorems 3.32
and 3.38. The fixed-label Perron values are not altered either, because they
are supplied by the normalized determinant evaluation of Proposition 3.56
before the secondary tail is formed.
For (iii), Proposition F.2 gives the forward and inverse Peter–Weyl transforms (179)–(180) between the normalized class profile bn (C) and the finite
list of edge sums. Theorem F.3 uses only those profiles to form the pointwise
correction and its tail, and Proposition F.4 substitutes that tail into a vanishing remainder factor. Thus the class-level secondary data and the finite
edge skeleton determine each other at every length, while all determinant
branches, cyclic-lift records, and product constants remain the already fixed
main-theorem objects.
□
Corollary F.6 (Product-level consequence of the secondary refinement).
Assume the full secondary hypotheses λ > 1, η < λ, R > λ1/2 , and Γsec < R.
The only information from the secondary spectral edge that is contributed to
the finite-group product theory is the vanishing tail factor in Proposition F.4.
In particular, after the preceding sections have fixed MC (A, τ ; G), KC , and
the non-scalar determinant coordinates (Fϱ (λ−1 ))ϱ̸=1 , the data Eϱ , sC (n),
and TC (N ) determine neither a new Perron-point coordinate nor a new
product constant.
Proof. Theorem F.3 expresses the secondary data only through sC (n) in the
pointwise remainder (181) and through the tail TC (N ) in (182). By Proposition F.2, these quantities are equivalent to the finite edge exponential sums
and contain no additional component outside the Peter–Weyl class profile
already used in the displayed identities. Proposition F.4 then substitutes
(182) into the class product and shows that TC (N ) → 0, so the limiting
constants remain exactly MC (A, τ ; G) and KC from Main Theorems II–III.
The determinant coordinates of KC are already identified by Theorem 3.41;
since the secondary terms occur only in the displayed vanishing tails, they
introduce no further product-theoretic datum.
□
Proposition F.7 (Kernel classification for the secondary refinement). Assume the full secondary hypotheses of Theorem F.3. Let Dmain denote the
finite determinant data {det(I − zAπ ) : π ∈ Irr(G)} together with the scalar
normalisation C(A) and the Adams–Möbius fixed-label transform used in
Main Theorems I–III. Then the conditional secondary refinement is classified, up to its effect on all Frobenius-class Mertens constants and Eulerproduct constants, by the zero object: for any two choices of secondary edge
data satisfying the same vanishing-tail hypotheses and attached to the same

156

HAOBO MA

Dmain , the resulting vectors
(MC (A, τ ; G))C ,

(KC )C ,

(Fπ (λ−1 ))π̸=1

are identical. The only non-zero secondary invariant left after the main constants are fixed is the finite-length tail profile (TC (N ))C,N , and this profile
lies in the kernel of the limiting map to product constants because TC (N ) → 0
for every conjugacy class C. Thus the appendix cannot be read as a second
spectral theorem or as an alternative determinant-recovery mechanism; it
is precisely a conditional remainder refinement downstream from Proposition 3.61.
Proof. Fix Dmain . Main Theorem I and Proposition 3.56 determine the nontrivial values Fπ (λ−1 ) from this determinant data and the stated branch
convention, before any edge set E1 or Eπ is introduced in this appendix.
Main Theorem II, in the body form Theorem 3.32, then determines each
MC (A, τ ; G). Main Theorem III, in the body form Theorem 3.38, determines each KC from those constants and the fixed-label Euler values.
Now attach any secondary edge data satisfying the hypotheses of Theorem F.3. Its pointwise contribution is the finite exponential
sum sC (n),
P
and its product-level contribution is the tail TC (N ) = n>N sC (n)/(nλn ).
By Proposition F.4, this tail tends to zero and appears in the product only
through the factor displayed in (183). Hence the limit defining the product
constant is unchanged. The finite-tail non-interference proposition Proposition 3.61 records the same conclusion: the tuples (MC )C and (KC )C are
unchanged, and the inverse Fourier recovery in Corollary 3.59 gives the same
recovered non-trivial vector (Fπ (λ−1 ))π̸=1 .
Finally, Proposition F.2 shows that the remaining secondary information
is equivalent, at each finite length, to the finite Peter–Weyl edge skeleton.
Since the preceding paragraph proves that its tails vanish in the limiting
product-constant map, this finite-length skeleton is a kernel datum for the
main constants rather than an additional coordinate of them. This proves
the asserted kernel classification.
□
Closure of the retained secondary package. Under the full secondary hypotheses λ > 1, η < λ, R > λ1/2 , and Γsec < R, the retained secondary
appendix is a conservative refinement of the three main finite-group
theorems in the following precise sense. The data of Main Theorems I–III
determine the primitive determinant coordinates, Perron-point values,
class Mertens constants, and Adams-corrected product constants exactly
as before; the additional secondary edge contributes only the pointwise
oscillating term of Theorem F.3, the vanishing product-tail factor of Proposition F.4, and the conditional recovery of the modulus R in Corollary F.8.
In particular, adding this appendix changes neither Πϱ (λ−1 ), Fϱ (λ−1 ),
MC (A, τ ; G), nor KC .
Main Theorem I fixes the primitive channels by finite Peter–Weyl determinant inversion, Main Theorem II evaluates their Perron-point class constants
under η < λ, and Main Theorem III converts those values into the Adamscorrected Euler-product constants. These steps are completed before any
use of the secondary separation assumptions R > λ1/2 and Γsec < R.

ADAMS–MOBIUS PRIMITIVE INVERSION

157

Under those additional hypotheses, Theorem F.3 gives the classwise pointwise secondary term and its harmonic tail. The insertion of that tail into
the product asymptotic is exactly Proposition F.4, where the secondary contribution appears through a factor tending to 1. Hence the limit defining
the class product constant remains the same. This is the non-interference
assertion of Proposition 3.61 applied to the secondary edge isolated in Theorem F.5. Finally, Corollary F.8 recovers only the modulus R from the residual classwise oscillation. That recovery uses the already subtracted Perron
term and therefore cannot alter the previously determined determinant coordinates or product constants. The four listed quantities are consequently
unchanged, while the secondary edge is retained as the stated conditional
refinement.
□
